# Egress and git access for agent pods

Status: design for owner review, revision 60, 2026-11-05. Revision 60 names the variables a tenant overlay can set, limits them to `internet` pools, and states the reservation's limit on those pools, after `tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md` decision 14. Revisions 58 and 59 apply the requests in `tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md` revisions 14 to 29. Revision 57 applies the requests in `tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md` revision 9. Revisions 52 to 55 change only the delegated child rule, the resolver hook, the `crossedFromEnvironment` envelope field, UG-1's dependency row, and a note under "Replaced pieces", after `tenant-scoping-identity-design.md` decision 14. Revisions 44 to 51 apply the hooks `user-git-identity-design.md` revision 11 and `user-credential-delivery-design.md` revision 8 request. **Revisions 47 and 48 apply the owner's git path decision (decision 44): git keeps its remote URLs, uses `netagent`'s egress endpoint as `http.proxy`, and `netagent` terminates VCS-covered hosts. Validation rounds before revision 47 measured the prior loopback path and stay as history. Claims that rested on them with no measurement under this path are listed under "Validation still owed".** The owner adopted the recommended decisions except where "Decisions" says otherwise, and reversed the deferral of level 1 replacement. Nothing in this document is built or staged. It replaces the "Git and egress design under owner review" handoff note in `gateway-runtime-comms-remediation.md` §10.2 as the current statement of the design, and it is the input to the change proposals listed under "Proposal split". This revision followed independent security and consistency reviews whose final stop check found no Major or higher issue. That check traced the last round's changes and was not an end-to-end read, and no production component or live provider write has run.

Ten validation rounds checked the design on 2026-11-01 and 2026-11-02, and twenty-five rounds of adversarial review plus a fact-check examined revisions 4 to 24 and 30 to 34 (see "Review record"). The first verified claims against vendor documentation, source code, the specification, and the Lenny tree. The second and third ran hands-on tests in throwaway containers and a throwaway kind cluster: Envoy 1.40.2 and 1.39.6, Squid 7.7, a Go proxy built on Smokescreen, git 2.49 with git-lfs 3.6, Kubernetes 1.35 on kind, and the client tools Maven, Gradle, `gh`, `glab`, npm, and pip. Each round also included research. The section "Validation record" lists what each round established, and "Validation still owed" lists what remains.

## Requirements

The owner set the following direction:

- Pods may have network access to named destinations. A pod needs some network path to clone a repository.
- Egress filtering applies to all HTTP traffic from pods that enable it, and git is one case of it.
- Runtimes can `git push`.
- Pools can have egress during warm-up, including cloning repositories.
- Lenny ships working defaults. A deployer can replace each default, or augment it with deployer-specific services.
- Different tenants can have different configurations, including different external proxies and git services. Tenant admins get as much flexibility as the platform's safety bounds allow.
- Pull requests and other host operations use the host's standard HTTP APIs through the egress route. Lenny adds no platform tool for them.
- The policy decision service runs separately from the gateway.
- Lenny does not adopt Goblet, which is archived (last commit 2021-07-14).
- This design covers egress and git. Related work lives in separate design documents, each with its own proposals: `security-oversight-design.md`, `forensics-evidence-preservation-design.md`, `tenant-scoping-identity-design.md`, `tenant-outbound-connector-design.md`, `user-git-identity-design.md`, and `user-credential-delivery-design.md`. A proposal here also carries work outside that scope only when adding it later would force rework of a stored record format, a wire contract, the on-disk layout, or a shipped enforcement point, and then it carries only the hook (see "Reserved for other designs").

## Security properties

The design holds these properties. Each later section states which component enforces them.

- P1. A pod reaches only the destinations its effective policy names.
- P2. Long-lived credentials never enter a pod, with these bounded exceptions: `netagent` memory holds a `stored` provider's read token for a fetch and, under the `storedTokenPush` opt-in, for a push (see "Credentials"); `netagent` memory holds a stored API secret or a user's token for an `inject` binding, which never names a git host a VCS pool covers when the value is stored; the agent-readable deliveries place a credential in the agent's reach under their opt-in, including a user's token under `userCredentialDelivery` (see "Credential delivery to runtimes" and `user-credential-delivery-design.md`); under level 1 replacement, `netagent` memory holds the identity's rotating proxy credential (see "Level 1 replacement"); and under the same gate as stored-token push, `netagent` memory holds a reused GitLab token (see **Push limits and commit identity**). Each opt-in sits behind its own gate (see "Credential delivery to runtimes", **Gates**), except a user's token under `inject`, which the owner's decision leaves to the tenant's binding write, because no process in the runtime or adapter container can read it.
- P3. Egress and VCS credentials are never readable by the agent UID. The exceptions are the credential binding deliveries that place a credential in the agent's reach by design: `file`, `federation`, and `metadata` (see "Credential delivery to runtimes"). A `userCredential` binding under `file` is such a delivery of a person's token, with limits `user-credential-delivery-design.md` states. Each needs its class's gate and a tenant opt-in (see "Credential delivery to runtimes", **Gates**). LLM credentials keep their existing delivery rules in §4.7.
- P4. One tenant's data and credentials never reach another tenant's pods.
- P5. Every allowed or denied egress connection is attributable to a pod, a pool, and a tenant once the pod is bound to one, and to a session where the pod model allows it.
- P6. Failure of any egress component blocks traffic.
- P7. A pod's load cannot remove another tenant's egress. Quotas per pod and per tenant bound tunnels, connection rate, and decision calls before any shared limit is reached.

These statements describe the default proxy and level 2 replacement. A deployment that selects level 1 replacement holds P1, P5, and P7 in a weaker form, which "Level 1 replacement" states.

## Architecture

```
  agent pod (one network namespace; NetworkPolicy applies to the whole pod)
 +------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
 | runtime container (agent UID)   adapter container      netagent container    |
 |  HTTPS_PROXY=127.0.0.1:3128 ------------------------> local egress endpoint -+--> lenny-egress (central proxy)
 |  git, http.proxy=:3128      ------------------------>  (terminates covered  --+--> git endpoint (lenny-git-mirror,
 |                                                         hosts)              |      deployer service, or upstream
 |                                                         | projected SA token  |      through lenny-egress)
 +---------------------------------------------------------+--------------------+
                                                           | binding (I7)        | credentials (I8)
                                                           v                     v
                                                   lenny-egress-authz <--scope-- Token Service (minting)
 lenny-egress --(ext_authz, per CONNECT)--> lenny-egress-authz
 lenny-egress --> internet, or --> upstream proxy chosen per tenant (forwarded CONNECT)
```

The design separates three responsibilities.

- **Network enforcement** happens in `lenny-egress` and the git endpoint. The pod's NetworkPolicy allows only those services, `lenny-egress-authz` (I7), the Token Service (I8), the `proxied` resolver, and the existing base destinations, each on its port. The runtime can reach those services directly, so the operative control is that every one of them acts only on a request that carries the pod's service account token, which only `netagent` holds. The NetworkPolicy bounds what the pod can address, and the token bounds what it can obtain.
- **Decisions** happen in `lenny-egress-authz`. It identifies the pod from its service account token and looks up the pod's current binding (pool, tenant, and sessions) from the records the controller and gateway already write.
- **Identity, credential use, and routing inside the pod** happen in `netagent`. This is a new Lenny container in every pod that uses the local endpoint, running under its own UID. It holds the pod's identity token and any upstream token, and it attaches them on the hops that leave the pod. It also enforces which repositories a credential may be used for, because it is the only component that sees both the plain git request and the credential.

### The `netagent` container

The local egress endpoint, and the git path on it, run in a dedicated container rather than in the existing adapter.

|                       | In the existing adapter                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | In a separate `netagent` container                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| :-------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Embedded runtimes     | The embedded runtime is the adapter, running at the agent UID. Every embedded runtime links the endpoints through `pkg/adapter`, must accept their rendered flags, and holds the egress identity and tokens at the agent UID, so P3 fails. | Embedded and sidecar pods get the same container. Embedded runtimes need no code and no new flags, and P3 holds in both models.                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Identity before claim | The adapter already mounts a projected service account token with the deployment's gateway audience (`podspec/sa_token.go`, 900 s), so an identity exists before claim in both designs. In the embedded model that token sits in the agent's container, so the agent could present the egress identity itself. | The container mounts its own projected tokens at pod creation, with audiences the agent's container never holds. The identity exists from the first second of the pod's life, independent of claim timing and pod model, and it is distinct from the adapter's. |
| New channels          | A lease handoff inside the adapter, plus a new delivery RPC timed before setup.                                                                                                                                                            | No intra-pod channel. `netagent` talks only to `lenny-egress-authz`, the Token Service, `lenny-egress`, and the git endpoint.                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Failure isolation     | A crash in proxy code takes down the adapter and the session.                                                                                                                                                                              | A crash restarts `netagent` only. Egress fails closed while it restarts.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Cost                  | No extra container.                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | One extra container per pod, including warm pods. It is a small Go process (a prototype on the git path peaked at 29 MB `memory.peak` over 64 MiB clones and a push, and at 41.7 MB under the request rules' earlier bounds). `applyResources` (`pkg/controller/sandbox/podspec/resources.go`) copies the pool's resource class onto every container, so the builder appends `netagent` after it runs, as it already does for the test-only egress-capture sidecar, and sets an explicit request from a flag. The egress-capture sidecar sets none and falls back to the namespace LimitRange. |
| Specification         | No new container rules.                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | A third reserved UID and a §13.1 container-identity row. The container does not mount `/run/lenny`, so the `lenny-cred-readers` boundary is unchanged.                                                                                                                                                    |

Recommendation: the separate container. The decisive point is that P3 holds for embedded runtimes: the egress identity and every upstream token stay in a container the agent UID cannot read.

**Identity.** The pod spec mounts a projected volume with two service account tokens into `netagent` only: audience `lenny-egress-<cluster-name>`, presented on I2, I3, I5, and I7, and audience `lenny-token-service-<cluster-name>`, which `netagent` presents to the Token Service directly on I8 (see "Egress decision service", **Credential requests**). The runtime and adapter containers do not mount either. The adapter keeps its existing gateway-audience token, and every validator rejects a token whose audience is not its own, so the gateway-audience token the embedded runtime holds at the agent UID buys nothing on the egress hops. The audiences carry the cluster name because §10.3 requires deployment-specific audiences, so a token from another Lenny deployment on the same cluster fails. Kubernetes binds each token to the pod (the JWT carries the pod name and UID) and rotates it; the design sets `expirationSeconds` to 600. `lenny-egress-authz` verifies the signature offline through the cluster's OIDC discovery keys, checks the pod UID against its watch of live pods, and requires the connection's peer address to be one of the pod's addresses (`status.podIPs`, which covers dual-stack) from the same watch, so a copied token is useless from any other source. The check assumes the pod-to-Service path is not source-translated; preflight verifies that kube-proxy does not masquerade pod sources (`masqueradeAll` and IPVS configurations), and the check is disabled with a warning where it would reject every pod. The mirror and the Token Service apply the same peer check. Server TLS protects the token in transit on these hops (see "Transport security"). R14 (agent-pod mTLS) does not change this model: its certificate lives in the adapter container, which the agent UID reaches in the embedded model, so it cannot serve as the egress identity.

Round 3 validated this on kind (Kubernetes 1.35):

- The runtime and adapter containers have no path to the token. §13.1 forbids `shareProcessNamespace` on every Lenny pod and the `lenny-pod-security` webhook rejects it, so the spike's shared-namespace run was a control for a misconfigured cluster: even then, `/proc/<pid>/root`, `environ`, and `fd` of `netagent` return permission denied. The protection rests on the separate mount and the distinct UID: a container at `netagent`'s UID read the token. `netagent`'s command line and mount table stay visible, so `netagent` takes no secret as an argument.
- A stdlib Go validator verified signature, issuer, audience, expiry, and the pod, node, and service account claims, and rejected a wrong audience, a token without a pod binding, a tampered payload, and an expired token. The default `system:service-account-issuer-discovery` binding was enough.
- After the pod was deleted, the validator rejected its unexpired token 55 to 72 ms after the watch delivered the deletion. The API server's TokenReview kept accepting the token for about 6 seconds after the pod was gone, so the offline check is faster than TokenReview.
- The kubelet rewrote the token at 80 to 88 percent of its 600-second lifetime with an atomic swap, and the lifetime was not extended.
- Anyone allowed to `create serviceaccounts/token` on the agent service account can mint a pod-bound token that the validator accepts, and anyone with `pods/ephemeralcontainers` or node access can read the mounted one. The chart grants none of these to a Lenny component, preflight gains a check that no role bound in the agent namespaces holds them, and the peer-address check above limits what a copied token can do.
- The protection is the mount boundary, not file modes. The pod's `fsGroup` (the `lenny-cred-readers` GID, `base_pod.go`) makes every volume group-readable, and the agent UID is in that group, so any volume mounted into both containers is readable by the agent. `netagent`'s token volume and its private emptyDir (CA key and state) are mounted into `netagent` only, and the `lenny-pod-security` and `lenny-ephemeral-container-cred-guard` webhooks extend their `/run/lenny` rules to those two volumes, so no other container or ephemeral container can mount them. Inside its private emptyDir, `netagent` creates its directories with mode 0700 and a 077 umask.

`netagent` runs as a native sidecar (an init container with `restartPolicy: Always`), so it starts before the runtime and stops after it. Round 3 confirmed that ordering, and that a `netagent` restart leaves the runtime and adapter running. Its startup probe calls a readiness endpoint rather than testing for a file, because after a restart a file check passed on the previous instance's file.

**Binding.** `lenny-egress-authz` resolves the pod UID to its Sandbox and pool, reads the tenant pin (`lenny.dev/tenant-id`) and any tenant ownership of the pool, and reads the pod's current session assignments from the session store. The feed from the gateway is explicit:

- The session store is the source of truth, read through the §12 transaction-mode pooler. Postgres `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY` is unavailable through that pooler (§12.6), and the pod registry polls for the same reason, so the design does not use it.
- The gateway pushes every binding change (bind, unbind, reclaim, and recycle) to every `lenny-egress-authz` replica, over a gRPC call on the gateway's egress PKI leaf to each endpoint of a headless Service, after the store write commits. Envoy spreads a pod's decisions across replicas and `netagent` subscribes to one of them, so a push to one replica would leave the others serving the old binding until their re-read. The push is a hint: a replica never updates its cache from the payload. It re-reads the session row and acts on what the store says, so a forged or replayed push changes nothing, and only the gateway identity is accepted on that call. The push never blocks the commit; the gateway retries each endpoint for a bounded time and then relies on the re-read.
- A decision for a pod whose binding is not in the cache reads through to the store before it answers, and every cached binding is re-read at a bounded age (30 seconds by default) whether or not a push arrived. A lost push therefore costs at most that bound, and the normal bound between a bind commit and the decision service seeing it is one gRPC round trip.
- Each binding carries the §7.3 coordination generation, and the decision service keeps the highest generation it has seen per session. A pod presenting a lower generation is refused, which closes replay of an old binding.
- A pod's session-scoped requests are honoured only when the session's current binding names that pod. §7.3 publishes no binding for a replacement pod until the `resuming → running` commit, yet `FinalizeWorkspace`, which now runs the clone in the pod, precedes that commit. The resume driver therefore writes a provisional binding (pod, session, and generation, with state `materializing`) before `FinalizeWorkspace`, and the commit promotes it. The provisional state grants read tokens for the session's `gitClone` sources and `inject` secrets for the session's bindings, because setup commands on a snapshotless rebuild use them as they did at finalize, and `inject` keeps those secrets out of the agent's reach. It serves binding definitions (hosts, pins, and CAs), and decisions use the session's policy, so setup commands have egress and the anchor is current before launch. Write tokens, `file`, `federation`, `metadata`, and any `userCredential` binding become available at the `running` commit, so a setup command that needs one of those on a rebuild fails and §6.2 sends the session to `awaiting_client_action`, which the documentation states. The binding lives outside the `pod_assignment` field, its write is fenced on the incarnation's `coordination_generation` in the step-2 transaction where the pod is already known, and every exit from `resuming` (the failure transition, the watchdog, and a mid-resume terminal) retires it in the same write that releases the pod, so a released pinned-idle pod carries no session grant. The §4.6.1 orphan-claim predicate keeps reading `pod_assignment`. That is a §7.3 change staged in proposal 2, and a tier 4 test resumes a session with a `gitClone` source.
- `netagent` receives the same changes through its I7 subscription to one replica, which is served from that replica's cache after the re-read. When the replica goes away (loss or rollout), `netagent` resubscribes to any replica, sends the last generation it saw, and receives the current binding from that replica's read-through; a resubscription whose generation matches the replica's cache is answered from cache, and resubscription has its own low rate limit (one per 10 seconds per pod), so a pod cannot drive store reads through it. Open tunnels stay open for a 30-second grace during resubscription and then close (P6). When a session ends or the binding changes, `netagent` closes that session's tunnels at once. On a concurrent-session pod it cannot attribute tunnels to a session, so tunnels there run to the pool's cap, which is a stated residual.

`lenny-egress-authz`, the Token Service, and `lenny-git-mirror` therefore read the session store through the pooler, with §13.2.4 rows. The Token Service reads only its own token and audit stores today (`cmd/lenny-token-service/stores.go`), so the session, pool, and binding reads are new for it. None of them holds a direct Postgres connection.

### Local egress endpoint (`netagent`)

`netagent` listens on `127.0.0.1:3128` as an HTTP CONNECT proxy that requires no client authentication. Every tool in the pod reaches the internet through it.

- The proxy variables are fixed values that are identical for every pod, so they are set at pod creation. In the sidecar model the supervisor sets them on the runtime process. In the embedded model they are container env.
- For each CONNECT, `netagent` opens a CONNECT to `lenny-egress` carrying `Proxy-Authorization: Basic` with the service account token as the password. 
- `netagent` does not resolve the target name and does not open TLS. It forwards the CONNECT authority unchanged, and nothing else from the client: it sends `Host` and its own `Proxy-Authorization`, and it drops every other header the agent sent, so an agent cannot inject a route header or an identity header. The decision overwrites the route header on every CONNECT regardless.
- `netagent` binds its loopback ports before the runtime starts and holds them. If a restart loses a port to a process the agent started, `netagent` fails its readiness probe, egress stays closed (P6), and the pod is reported unhealthy and retired. The adapter's own git traffic (the materialization clone) uses the same port and the same bundle. A process standing on the port cannot pose as a covered host to the adapter, because it lacks the per-pod CA's key, and the adapter holds no credential to give it.
- `netagent` refuses every CONNECT and absolute-form request until it holds its first I7 answer after any start, including the warm answer and a restart. CONNECT got 503 for covered, bound, and opaque hosts before the answer and again after a restart **[measured]**.
- Before any binding exists, the pod's effective policy is the pool's warm-up policy. Warm-up egress therefore works for pod-warm, SDK-warm, and service-mode pods alike.
- `netagent`'s listeners set a head read deadline, 10 seconds by default, and refuse a bare LF in a request head at once. A prototype held a bare-LF request for 20 seconds with no deadline (recorded in `user-git-identity-design.md`).

### Central egress proxy (`lenny-egress`)

`lenny-egress` is a Kubernetes Deployment, a replicated set of identical stateless pods, in the `lenny-system` namespace where the gateway and Token Service already run. It runs Envoy 1.39.6 or later (tested on 1.40.2 and 1.39.6), configured by a static bootstrap that the Helm chart renders. Lenny writes no proxy code.

The second spike verified each of these behaviours on both versions:

- It terminates HTTP/1.1 CONNECT and reaches the target through the dynamic forward proxy.
- It sends an ext_authz gRPC check to `lenny-egress-authz` for every CONNECT. The check carries the method, the authority (host and port), all request headers including `Proxy-Authorization`, and the peer address. A deny returns 403. A 407 with `Proxy-Authenticate: Basic` is available for clients that need a challenge. If `lenny-egress-authz` is unreachable, Envoy returns 503 and opens no tunnel (P6).
- It writes the decision metadata that ext_authz returns (decision identifier, tenant, pool, session, and rule) into a JSON access log, with the upstream address, the upstream source port, and bytes in each direction (the fields "Evidence streams" names), for allows and for denies. Long tunnels log at establishment and at an interval, as well as at close.
- `DnsCacheConfig.resolved_address_filter` refuses targets that resolve to private, loopback, link-local, metadata, or cluster ranges. It also covers IP literals, because literals pass through the same resolution step. The range list must include `::ffff:0:0/96` (IPv4-mapped) and `64:ff9b::/96` (NAT64). Without them, `[::ffff:127.0.0.1]` passes the filter. The list also carries the Azure wireserver `168.63.129.16/32`, `0.0.0.0/8`, `100.64.0.0/10`, `198.18.0.0/15`, multicast, and `::/128`, and refuses IPv6 zone identifiers. The list has no exemptions: a private destination a tenant is allowed to reach never passes through resolution (see "Egress decision service", **Private destinations**). The ext_authz filter runs before the dynamic forward proxy filter, so a refused authority is never resolved, which closes a DNS side channel for denied names. A conformance test pins the list and the filter order.
- `max_stream_duration` ends active, idle, and client-only CONNECT tunnels at the configured time (5.0 seconds in the test, with `delayed_close_timeout` lowered). `max_connection_duration` does not end tunnels, and the design does not use it.
- **Tunnel lifetime.** `max_stream_duration` is route configuration in the static bootstrap, so the chart renders a fixed menu of routes (15 minutes, 1 hour, 2 hours, and 8 hours by default, as Helm values) and a pool names one of them. The decision selects that route, so the cap is a pool setting without xDS: an LLM pool can carry 2 hours while a coding pool carries 15 minutes. A pod whose `netagent` is gone keeps a tunnel until its pool's cap, attributed to the session that opened it. That is the residual of Envoy having no per-tunnel kill, and the per-pool cap bounds it.
- It chains a tunnel to an upstream proxy chosen per request. The decision response sets a route header and returns the upstream address in dynamic metadata. An `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster then dials that address, and Envoy forwards the CONNECT with an injected `Proxy-Authorization` or identity headers. The upstream proxy received only the injected credential, and the pod's token did not reach it. Changing a tenant's upstream needs no Envoy reload.
- Throughput: four parallel 4 GB downloads completed at about 2.7 GB/s in total, with Envoy at 130% CPU and under 80 MiB of memory.

The `http_11_proxy` transport socket does not chain raw CONNECT tunnels and is not used. An `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster that reads the upstream from a request header also failed, so the upstream address always comes from ext_authz dynamic metadata.

The third round repeated the baseline on both versions and added these results:

- **ext_authz in HTTP mode** works only when Envoy reaches the decision service over HTTP/2 (h2c). Over HTTP/1.1, Envoy sends the check with method `CONNECT` and every request fails with 503. `Proxy-Authorization` reaches the check only when `allowed_headers` lists it, and the peer address needs `headers_to_add` with `%DOWNSTREAM_REMOTE_ADDRESS%`. The gRPC form needs neither and remains the default.
- **The pod's credential on chained routes.** Unless the decision service removes it (`x-envoy-auth-headers-to-remove: proxy-authorization` in HTTP mode, or the equivalent header mutation in gRPC mode), Envoy forwards the pod's `Proxy-Authorization` to the tenant's upstream proxy. `lenny-egress-authz` removes it on every chained route, and a conformance test pins that.
- **Upstream proxy replies.** By default a chained upstream's 407 or 403 reaches the pod with its `Proxy-Authenticate` headers and body. The `custom_response` filter, matched per route, maps them to a fixed 502 or 403 with no upstream headers. `response_headers_to_remove` strips headers but passes the body, and `local_reply_config` does not apply to upstream replies.
- **IPv6.** IPv6-only targets work by literal and by AAAA-only names. `dns_lookup_family: AUTO` prefers IPv6 for dual-stack names, and `V4_PREFERRED` prefers IPv4. The address filter blocks ULA, link-local, loopback, mapped, and NAT64 addresses.
- **DNS rebinding.** The address filter runs on every resolution and refresh, and no private address was ever contacted. A name that rebinds to a private address keeps its last public address until `host_ttl` expires and is then refused. A tunnel that is already open keeps its address. Envoy flags the filter's range type as work in progress and outside its threat model at startup. The `lenny-egress` NetworkPolicy and the decision service's literal checks back it up.
- **Load** (2 CPUs, `--concurrency 2`). 1,000 concurrent tunnels had a CONNECT setup latency of 2.4 ms at p50 and 10.8 ms at p99, at 22% CPU and about 54 KiB per tunnel. 2,000 new CONNECTs per second, each with a decision call, had a p99 of 8.7 ms at 59% CPU. The default cluster circuit breaker caps each cluster at 1,024 concurrent tunnels and then queues and fails with `503 UO`, so the chart raises the breakers on every tunnel cluster.
- **A slow decision service** adds its latency to each CONNECT. Beyond the ext_authz timeout, every CONNECT fails closed at the timeout. With the decision service stopped, CONNECTs got 503 after about 0.5 s, and service recovered about 2 s after restart. The chart sets the ext_authz timeout to 250 to 500 ms, a decision-service connect timeout of about 250 ms, a breaker on the decision-service cluster sized to the CONNECT rate times the timeout, and a global downstream connection limit.
- **Close timing.** The default 1 s `delayed_close_timeout` delays the close of a tunnel that `max_stream_duration` ends, and the chart lowers it.
- **Decision-service resilience** (round 5, 500 CONNECTs per second against two decision endpoints). With HTTP/2 keepalive (1 s interval and timeout), outlier detection on local-origin failures, an active gRPC health check that ejects in about 1 s and readmits only on a passing check, and one retry on `previous_hosts`, killing one endpoint and freezing one with `SIGSTOP` each caused zero failed CONNECTs out of 30,000. Without the health check, the base configuration lost about 1% on a kill, mostly when the ejected endpoint was probed with live traffic after its ejection expired, and about 2% on a freeze. One pitfall: Envoy uses the retry policy's `max_interval` as the per-try timeout, so it must equal the per-try budget (0.4 s); at 50 ms it failed half of all CONNECTs.
- **Long streams** (round 5). 200 tunnels each carrying one chunk per second ran 20 minutes with none cut and Envoy's memory flat at about 27 to 33 MiB, with `stream_idle_timeout` 300 s, `idle_timeout` 3600 s, `max_stream_duration` 7200 s, and periodic access-log entries every 60 s. Two silent tunnels were cut at exactly 300 s by the idle timeout, as intended, and with a 120 s cap all 200 ended at 120 s with a clean close.
- **Decision latency** (round 5). Envoy has no ext_authz response cache, so caching lives in the decision service. At 1,000 CONNECTs per second with 50 ms decisions, Envoy added about 1.6 ms and held about 50 checks in flight per endpoint, so the decision service is sized at rate times latency times two.
- **Drain** (round 5). `POST /drain_listeners?graceful` with `--drain-time-s 20` kept accepting CONNECTs for 20 s and reset every tunnel between two thirds of the drain time and the drain time minus the HTTP connection manager's 5 s drain timeout, with a clean close. `/ready` stays 200 during the drain, so the Service endpoint must be withdrawn separately; a plain `SIGTERM` closes every tunnel at once. The chart's preStop hook calls the graceful drain and sleeps for the drain time, with `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` above it.

Envoy marks CONNECT alpha in a proto comment on `RouteMatch.connect_matcher` and `UpgradeConfig.connect_config` ("Note that CONNECT support is currently considered alpha in Envoy."), with a TODO to replace it with a formal tag. `upgrades.rst` marks only CONNECT-UDP and raw UDP tunnelling as alpha, and Envoy logged no alpha warning at runtime. The dynamic forward proxy, ext_authz, and `tcp_proxy` are marked stable. Envoy terminating CONNECT runs in production in Istio ambient waypoints (HBONE, HTTP/2 CONNECT) and in Google Cloud Secure Web Proxy, which is built on an Envoy proxy pool and accepts HTTP CONNECT. Recent advisories touch CONNECT and the dynamic forward proxy, among them CVE-2025-64763 (early CONNECT data in TCP proxy mode) and CVE-2025-54588 (a use-after-free in the DNS cache). The chart therefore pins a patched Envoy release and the project tracks Envoy security advisories. A conformance suite in the Lenny test tiers covers every behaviour above.

Its own egress NetworkPolicy allows the public internet minus `egressCIDRs.excludePrivate`, cluster CIDRs, and IMDS, using the same rule as the gateway's upstream rule (§13.2.5, NET-057). It also allows the registered upstream proxies and private git services listed in Helm values, which are the only private addresses the decision can name. That policy is the backstop for the `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster, which dials any address the decision names.

### Transport security

Every pod-to-platform hop and every hop that carries a pod token uses TLS. No hop needs a pod client certificate, and the certificates come from a PKI of their own, so none of this depends on R14.

| Hop | Protection | What it protects |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| I2, `netagent` to the central proxy | Server TLS, verified by `netagent` against the egress CA bundle and the proxy's Service name | The service account token, or the level 1 credential |
| I5, `netagent` to the mirror | Server TLS, verified the same way | The token and private repository contents |
| I7, `netagent` to the decision service | Server TLS, verified the same way | The token and binding data |
| I8, `netagent` to the Token Service | Server TLS, as already stated | Tokens and binding secrets |
| I3 and the access log stream, Envoy to the decision service | mTLS. Envoy verifies the decision service's name, and the decision service admits only an allowlist of client SANs (`lenny-egress` and each egress group, the mirror, and the Token Service, plus the gateway and a level 2 proxy where they call I3). | The pod tokens every check carries, and the decision API itself, which a compromised mirror fetch could otherwise query with a forged pod UID to read tenant tags |

The gateway resolver and the mirror's upstream fetches use the same TLS listener on `lenny-egress`. I1 and the runtime's traffic to `netagent` stay on loopback, where a CONNECT is in plaintext and git's request inside it is TLS under the per-pod CA. A level 2 proxy must serve TLS on I2, as the level 1 contract already requires, and a chained upstream (I6) stays as the deployer's upstream defines it.

**I3 operations by identity.** A flat allowlist is not enough, because a full check returns a route with an upstream address and credential headers, and a compromised mirror parent could otherwise call I3 directly, assert a tenant, and receive that tenant's upstream proxy credential. The decision service binds each client SAN to the operations it may call:

| Client SAN | Operations |
| :-- | :-- |
| `lenny-egress`, each egress group, and a level 2 proxy | The full check, including the route and credential headers |
| The mirror, and the gateway at level 1 | A git decision for an asserted tenant: allow or deny, and a private endpoint's address at level 1. The mirror also makes its second-gate check on a caller's token. Neither returns credential headers. |
| The Token Service | The scope decision, with no route, and a mint-path decision for a host whose route is not `direct` |
| Egress evidence | None: `lenny-evidence` has its own API, and its allowlist binds each SAN to its streams |

The mirror's and the gateway's upstream traffic still goes through `lenny-egress`, which injects any upstream credential itself, so they never see it. The decision service honours the mirror's tenant assertion on that path only when that tenant's git route names the mirror for the host, as the Token Service's I8 check already requires, and the Token Service applies the same check before it releases a level 1 service identity to the mirror. The direct I3 git decision applies the same check. A compromised mirror parent can therefore fetch only through the routes of tenants that routed through it.

**The egress PKI.** The existing PKI is gated by `mtls.enabled` (`mtls-pki.yaml`), which a deployment using a service mesh turns off, and the Kind overlay keeps it off until R14 gives agent pods certificates. These hops therefore have their own PKI, which the chart renders whenever `netagent` is enabled, whatever `mtls.enabled` says, because I7 and I8 need it even with a replacement proxy and no mirror: a self-signed bootstrap, an `egress` CA, and a namespaced Issuer, as `mtls-pki.yaml` does for the other. A deployment without cert-manager supplies the Secrets by name, and the chart refuses to render otherwise. The chart renders a leaf for `lenny-egress` and one for each egress group, so each group has its own Service name, and leaves for the decision service, the mirror, the Token Service, `lenny-evidence`, the gateway, the controller, at level 1 `lenny-ops`, and later `lenny-siem-forwarder` from `security-oversight-design.md`. Each leaf's SAN is its exact Service DNS name, with no wildcard. The gateway's leaf serves the resolver's trust and the binding push, whose listener admits only that SAN, so neither depends on `lenny-mtls-ca`. A mesh's mTLS, where present, wraps this TLS rather than replacing it. R14 may decide to merge the two PKIs if it makes the `mtls.enabled` PKI always on, and nothing in this design prevents or requires that. A deployer who enforces `restricted` on `lenny-system` places the mirror in its own `lenny-mirror` namespace rather than lowering that namespace to `baseline`. That placement needs an issuer in the namespace, which proposal 5 settles.

**The CA bundle for pods.** `netagent` verifies against a bundle that holds only the egress CA, its previous CA during a rotation, and for a replacement proxy the CA that Helm names. It never uses the TLS interception bundle, which holds public roots, the deployer's CA, and any `tenantOverride.caBundle`, because those CAs could then impersonate a platform service. The CA's key sits in a Secret in `lenny-system`, which no agent pod can read. `lenny-ops` and the controller are inside this PKI's trust boundary, as they are for the existing one: `lenny-ops` can read Secrets in `lenny-system`, create Jobs that mount them, and patch Certificates, and the controller can create pods. Either, if compromised, can obtain the CA's key or a leaf with another component's SAN, and through it a tenant's upstream credential from the full I3 check. This is a stated residual, and `security-oversight-design.md` (SO-7) owns narrowing those rights. The controller reads the public `ca.crt` from its own leaf's mount and writes a `lenny-egress-ca` ConfigMap to each agent namespace, to `lenny-system`, and to the mirror's namespace where it has its own, with a new RBAC row. Each pod mounts it into `netagent` only, without `subPath`, so kubelet updates reach warm pods, and `netagent` reads the bundle for each new connection. An endpoint registration (a git endpoint or an upstream) or a credential binding can carry its own CA, which `netagent`, the mirror, the resolver, and the Token Service use only to validate that endpoint or the binding's hosts, and which reaches `netagent` from the Token Service on I8. It needs no pod trust, so it works on a shared pool, and a tenant can register one for a public-address endpoint. A level 1 or level 2 proxy gets its CA and server name from `centralProxy.caBundle` and `centralProxy.serverName`, under a separate key of the ConfigMap that only the proxy hop reads, so a deployer's CA is never trusted for I5, I7, or I8.

**CA rotation.** Every component reads trust from `lenny-egress-ca` rather than from its own leaf's `ca.crt`, so a server's leaf can re-issue under a new CA without breaking a client that has not yet re-issued its own. A routine CA renewal keeps the key (`rotationPolicy: Never`), so existing chains still verify. A key rotation runs in a fixed order, and it needs a second CA Certificate and Secret, because the Issuer's `secretName` is what switches. A Helm value carries both the new and the outgoing CA, and the controller adds them to every bundle whatever its own leaf's `ca.crt` says. The rollout waits for the kubelet to deliver them, which took up to 80.8 seconds in round 10 and is the sync period rather than a bound. The Issuer then switches to the new CA. A switch re-issues nothing by itself, so every leaf is renewed, with `cmctl renew` or by waiting out its window. The value is cleared only after every re-issued leaf has been delivered to its pod. Round 10 ran this with 15 connections per second: no connection failed in 11,700 when the order waited for delivery, 817 of 6,300 failed over 55 seconds when the value was cleared as soon as the Secrets had re-issued, and a routine renewal failed none of 3,150.

Round 7 compared TLS with plaintext on one harness, on Envoy 1.40.2 and 1.39.6 for the feature matrix. Allow, deny, forwarded chaining with an injected credential, the server-name check through the internal listener, every SSRF case, IPv6, and fail-closed behaviour gave the same outcomes. So did the raw tunnel, the tunnel lifetime cap, and a drain with 100 open tunnels. With decision endpoints killed and frozen at 500 CONNECTs per second, 0 of 30,000 failed with TLS on I2, and 0 of 30,000 with TLS on I2 and mTLS on I3. The decision service refused a client outside the allowlist and one with no certificate, and Envoy failed closed against an untrusted or a plaintext decision service. The stand-in for the mirror served a full clone of 422,967 objects in 22 s with and without TLS, with identical shallow, partial, deepen, and progress behaviour.

The chart and the design carry these rules from that round:

- **CPU limits on `netagent`.** TLS costs `netagent` the kernel's zero-copy path, moving its CPU from 0.16 to 0.81 CPU-seconds per GiB. Under a 50m hard CPU limit, throughput fell from 329 to 34 MB/s, under 250m from 610 to 497 MB/s, and at 1 CPU or with no limit it did not change. `applyResources` stamps a pool's resource class onto every Lenny container, so `netagent` is excluded from it, as the test sidecar is, and has resources of its own: a CPU request, and no CPU limit of its own, so the namespace LimitRange's default of 2 CPUs applies. The chart refuses a `netagent` limit below 250m, in its own value and in a per-namespace LimitRange override.
- **Certificate reload.** Envoy loads its certificate through SDS on a watched directory, which needs `node.id` and `node.cluster` in the bootstrap. A symlink swap in the Secret-mount form rotated the certificate with 200 tunnels open and none closed. Invalid material kept the last good certificate.
- **Readiness needs a handshake.** Envoy reports `LIVE` on `/ready` while refusing every connection in each of these states at start: a missing directory, an empty mount, missing keys, and invalid material. With a missing directory it does not recover when the certificate appears. Once Envoy's I3 client certificate also comes through SDS, invalid material at start makes Envoy exit and recover through back-off restarts, and the kubelet keeps the pod not Ready either way. The chart therefore adds a small TLS listener that shares the SDS secret and answers `/healthz` with 200, and the readiness probe uses it with `scheme: HTTPS`, which skips verification but requires a handshake. Round 7 confirmed that listener fails in all four states and passes with a valid certificate, and round 10 confirmed it with the kubelet's own probe against a real Secret, with recovery in 16 to 38 seconds once a valid Secret appeared. No pod-side rule reaches it. The chart also makes the Secret mount non-optional, so an absent Secret keeps the container from starting. The Go components fail at start on invalid material and keep their last good certificate on a failed reload.
- **Expiry is an outage.** Clients refuse an expired, wrong-name, or untrusted certificate, and they refuse TLS 1.1, so every failure fails closed, and an expired certificate stops all egress through that replica. The readiness probe skips verification, so it passes an expired leaf, and Envoy's day-granular expiry stat reads 0 on a healthy 24-hour leaf, so neither can drive the alert. The chart extends `CertExpiryImminent` (`lenny_cert_expiry_seconds`) to the new Certificates, and a rejected reload raises an alert on `sds.server.key_rotation_failed`. `lenny-ctl doctor`'s `certManagerExpiring` remedy deletes the Secret of any Certificate it matches, which includes every 24-hour leaf, and a CA's Secret holds its key. The egress Certificates therefore carry `lenny.dev/doctor-optout`, a hard skip, until SO-6 in `security-oversight-design.md` fixes the remedy.
- **CA rotation.** A bundle holding the old and new CAs verified a server under either, and round 10 ran the whole order below.
- **Reload on I3.** Envoy reloads its I3 client certificate through SDS, and a new certificate reaches the peer only on a new connection, so the I3 cluster sets a `max_connection_duration` below the leaf's renewal window. The decision service checks its SAN allowlist in Go's `VerifyConnection`, because `VerifyPeerCertificate` does not run on resumed handshakes, which were 41 of 44 in round 10.
- **Sizing.** CONNECT setup at p50 did not change. The p99 went from 5.4 to 13 ms at 1,000 CONNECTs per second and from 9.7 to between 21 and 47 ms at 2,000. Envoy's CPU at 2,000 per second went from 60% to about 82% of one core, its bulk cost from 0.59 to 0.87 CPU-seconds per GiB, and its memory by about 9 KiB per tunnel. Bulk throughput did not change, at about 1.3 GB/s in total, where the client side was the limit. TLS session resumption worked (398 of 400 handshakes). On I7, 2,000 streams used 136 MiB against 76 MiB, about 30 KiB more per stream, and after a server restart all 2,000 returned in 2 s against 3 s at 1 CPU, and in 9 s against 5 s at 0.25 CPU, with no failed stream. mTLS on I3 added no measurable latency: with no faults the p99 at 500 per second was 2.8 ms against 10.7 ms with I3 in plaintext, and under faults 3.1 ms, with the worst case at about 420 ms either way. One earlier run under faults showed 166 ms, so these tail figures vary between runs by more than the cost of TLS.

### Default proxy choice

| Candidate                     | Result in the hands-on spike                                                                                                                                                                                | Lenny code                                     | Main trade-off                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| :---------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Envoy 1.40                    | Passed CONNECT, per-connection decisions, SSRF filtering, the lifetime cap, and per-tenant chaining                                                                                                         | None (configuration only)                      | No per-tunnel kill. `netagent` closes tunnels in the pod, and `max_stream_duration` bounds a misbehaving pod.                                                                                             |
| Go proxy on Smokescreen       | Passed every test, including the lifetime cap and kill by lease within a second                                                                                                                             | About 200 lines in the spike, plus hardening   | Lenny owns a security-critical proxy and tracks a library with no tagged releases. The spike found two pitfalls Lenny code must close: a client header that selects an upstream, and a 407 on every deny. |
| Squid 7.7 with a Lenny helper | Passed everything except the lifetime cap: `client_lifetime` did not end active tunnels                                                                                                                     | A helper of about 30 lines, plus configuration | Revocation waits for the helper cache TTL. Per-tenant upstream credentials are static configuration lines. Squid has a heavy CVE history.                                                                 |
| Others                        | HAProxy, Caddy forwardproxy, nginx with the CONNECT module, Pomerium, Tinyproxy, mitmproxy, Apache Traffic Server, and the agent sandbox proxies each failed one or more requirements, or were unmaintained | n/a                                            | Ruled out. agentgateway 1.7 merits a later look.                                                                                                                                                          |

Envoy is the recommended default because it met every requirement with configuration alone, it implements the decision API natively over a widely used wire protocol (ext_authz, also used by Istio's CUSTOM authorization), and deployers who replace the default at level 2 can use the same protocol. The missing per-tunnel kill is covered by `netagent` and the lifetime cap. The Smokescreen-based proxy is the fallback if Envoy's CONNECT support regresses.

### Egress decision service (`lenny-egress-authz`)

`lenny-egress-authz` is a Lenny-built Deployment that serves the decision API (I3) and the pod binding API (I7). Credentials come from the Token Service on I8, with the decision service as one of two independent gates.

- It validates the caller's service account token and resolves the pod's binding.
- It computes the effective policy. The rules are under "Effective policy".
- It returns allow or deny, the route (`direct` or a named upstream with its address and credential or identity headers), the route's tunnel cap, and the attribution tags.
- It enforces quotas (P7). Rates (new CONNECTs and decision calls per second, per pod and per tenant) are counted in the decision service, which sees every open. Concurrency needs a close signal that ext_authz lacks, so `netagent` enforces the per-pod concurrent-tunnel quota in the pod, and Envoy streams its access log to the decision service through its gRPC access log service, which gives the per-tenant concurrency count and the tunnel-close visibility the audit trail needs. That stream is lossy: Envoy buffers and drops when the stream is down or full, and a restarting Envoy emits no close for its open tunnels. The periodic long-tunnel log entries therefore serve as heartbeats; a counted tunnel with no record for three intervals expires; the count resets per Envoy instance when the stream's identity changes; and the per-tenant concurrency quota is approximate, with an alert when the count and the per-pod sums disagree. The per-pod limit in `netagent` is the exact bound. Unbound warm pods count against a per-pool bucket. The chart refuses a configuration whose tenant quotas sum to more than Envoy's global downstream limit and tunnel breakers, and the defaults leave room for warm-up parallelism (`npm ci`) and for `maxConcurrentSessions` times the LLM streams a session holds. The mirror caps `upload-pack` concurrency per tenant.
- It rejects IP-literal targets in non-canonical forms (for example `0177.0.0.1` or `2130706433`) and private literals, because the proxy and the decision service can interpret those forms differently.
- It records every decision for audit (P5), in the `egress.decision` stream (see "Evidence streams").
- It caches decisions per pod and host for a short TTL (30 seconds by default). The cache holds the policy outcome, and a route target's kind can resolve its address and credential per call, because a cached connector grant or replica would be stale. The cache key includes the terminated marker, so an unmarked CONNECT to a pinned host cannot reuse a marked allow, and a policy version that every tenant policy, host set, route, overlay, and binding write increments, so an edit takes effect at the next decision rather than at TTL expiry, and a pod's entries are dropped when its binding changes.
- It compares an asserted route, on a call that carries a user token or a client secret: `netagent` after a `userCredential` release or under `allowRuntimeHeldCredentials`, and the Token Service on a link provider call. An App or project token mint carries neither, and it keeps the decision it has today, on the path the fetch takes, with no route assertion and no refusal for lacking one. The caller sends, in a header on the CONNECT as the terminated marker is sent, the list it matched, `egress`, `git`, or `default`, and a digest of the route from the token trust record it compared. A request's class, git path or API, tries its own list, then the other list, then the platform default, in the order `user-credential-delivery-design.md` writes out, in both the assertion and the decision, so a host with only a git route, as in "A self-hosted GitLab on a private address", and a host with only an egress route, as on an outbound connector, both work **[design]**. The decision service's git list omits a git route to `direct` or the mirror, as the record does. It refuses when the first match in the named list differs, including on a cached decision, so a route edit reaching it first stops the token rather than redirecting it. The cache key includes the asserted list, because a host with both lists has two routes, and an unasserted CONNECT's key does not change. A level 2 proxy that drops the header gets no comparison, and its conformance row is the control. A provider in its `deleting` state, or suspended by a guardrail, still declares its endpoints for revocation. `netagent` strips this header from agent input, and at level 1 its I7 decision call carries the same assertion. `netagent` keys its upstream pool on the list and the route digest, and a route write closes pooled upstream connections to the hosts it changes **[design]**, because idle pooled connections carried git past a route edit in a run, on a stand-in with no per-client-CONNECT decision **[measured]**. `user-credential-delivery-design.md` defines the record.
- It answers the Token Service's scope checks (see **Credential requests** below). It never holds a pod's Token Service token and never requests a credential itself, so a compromised decision service can misdecide, but it cannot mint. With `tenant-outbound-connector-design.md`'s OC-1 it also holds a grant signing key, and that design states the bound.

**Credential requests.** `netagent` requests upstream git tokens, binding secrets, federation JWTs, and `metadata` credentials from the Token Service directly, on I8, presenting its `lenny-token-service` audience token. Two components gate every mint independently, and each reads its inputs itself:

- The Token Service verifies the pod token offline against the cluster issuer and resolves the pod itself through the shared `pkg/binding` package, which both components run as one implementation with two independent instances. The package is Kubernetes-backed for identity: an informer over the agent namespaces and `lenny-system` (the latter for the mirror and gateway service identities) gives the pod UID, the `lenny.dev/tenant-id` label (the authoritative tenant pin, `tenantpin.go`), the Sandbox and its pool, and `status.podIPs` for the peer-address check. The informer stores a projection of each pod (UID, name, namespace, labels, `podIPs`, and phase) rather than whole Pod objects, so three components caching the agent pods at the §17 scale stay small. Before the informer has synced, every pod is unknown and every request fails closed; on API server loss client-go serves its last cache, so a deleted pod's token stays valid until its expiry (600 s), which is the stated bound. The same outage stops the kubelet from renewing projected tokens, so every pod's egress fails closed about 600 s into it, the Kubernetes minimum for `expirationSeconds`; that is a stated residual with an alert, and it bounds how long existing pods keep working during an API server outage, and the component reports the stale cache in its readiness detail without failing the Token Service's readiness for the gateway lease path (F-4.3.3). It reads the pool record (owner tenant and overlay) and the session binding, including the provisional one, from the stores through the pooler, and the binding definitions (the VCS pool, the credential binding with its `hosts` and `attach` template, and the session's writable set) from the same stores. `agent_pod_state` is not an identity source: its `tenant_id` is a convenience column set on claim, as its schema comment says. The Token Service therefore gains `list` and `watch` on pods in the agent namespaces and `lenny-system`, and a pooler read of the pool and session stores (§13.2.4), and it enforces the writable set, the tenant allowlist, and the peer check from its own reads.
- It then asks `lenny-egress-authz` on I3 for the scope decision. That I3 call carries the verified pod UID, the observed peer address, and the requested scope, and never the pod's token, so a compromised decision service collects nothing it can replay. The decision returns allow or deny and the attribution tags, and no secret; tenant identity never comes from I3 in either component. The mint happens only when both gates agree, and each has derived the tenant from Kubernetes on its own.
- The I8 response carries the binding's authoritative `hosts` and `attach` template, and any resolved `requestRules`, from the Token Service's own read, and `netagent` gates injection, and TLS termination for those hosts, on that set only. The copy of the bindings in the I7 subscription is informational, so a compromised decision service cannot widen the hosts a secret travels to, and cannot widen a write mint within a tenant either. It can misdecide a CONNECT, which is the bound of a compromised policy decision point. The one input I7 decides is the covered set, because a warm or public-only pod makes no I8 call. A compromised decision service can remove a host from that set, and it also evaluates the terminated marker, so nothing stops it there: git to that host then goes as an opaque tunnel, a token the agent holds reaches it, and git enforcement is off for that pod. No mint travels on that path, because `netagent` asks I8 only on the git path. This is the same bound, and pins arrive on I8, so a pinned host stays terminated.

- The gateway stamps `lenny.dev/tenant-id` at bind, so a component's informer can see the label after the pod's first request arrives. `pkg/binding` therefore reports a pod whose session binding exists and whose label has not yet appeared as not yet visible, which it keeps distinct from an unknown pod. Every `pkg/binding` instance (the Token Service, the decision service, and the mirror) answers a retryable error for that state, `netagent` retries with backoff for up to 5 seconds, and nothing is minted or allowed until the label is seen. A label that never appears fails closed when the retry bound ends. Service mode has no claim and no session binding (§5.2), and it also has no arbiter today: `tenantaffinity.Router` is in-memory per gateway replica and picks an unpinned pod with no shared record, and `statelessproxy` treats a label failure as best effort and proxies anyway. Tenants on two replicas can therefore pin one pod, and the second tenant's request reaches a pod labelled for the first, under the first tenant's policy and `always` bindings, which breaks P4. The design requires a durable pin record:

  - **Arbitration.** The pod's label is the authority once it exists, and the record arbitrates between replicas for an unlabelled pod. The router never selects a pod labelled for another tenant. For an unlabelled pod, a conditional insert keyed on pod UID in the session store decides the tenant. A replica that loses to another tenant reroutes the request to another pod, a replica that loses to the same tenant adopts the pin, and a replica that cannot write aborts the request. A webhook refusal of the stamp counts as lost arbitration, because it means a record was lost after the label was set: the replica removes its own record and reroutes.
  - **Router.** The router and the labeler key on pod UID, which the pod lister provides, because the router polls every 5 seconds and an IP reused between polls would otherwise leave a replica's pin on a new pod with no insert. It reads the tenant's records on a route miss, so a gateway rollout does not strand the tenant's pinned pods. A replica stops routing on a snapshot older than 30 seconds, where today a failed list keeps the last one indefinitely.
  - **Dial.** The gateway dials the pod's IP over plain HTTP, so a UID key alone cannot protect against an IP that moved inside the snapshot's age. No component on today's path can check that: `statelessproxy` dials the runtime's own port, no Go file imports `pkg/adapter/statelessslot`, and the chart's gateway rows admit only the adapter gRPC port, so an enforcing CNI drops this dial. The design adds a service-mode listener in `pkg/adapter`, in the sidecar and embedded models alike, that fronts the runtime and carries the `statelessslot` accounting. Each request carries the expected pod UID, the listener compares it with its own UID from the downward API and refuses a mismatch, and the gateway then reroutes. The gateway dials that listener's port, and the agent ingress row, the gateway egress row, and the §28 register gain it. This catches an IP that moved to an honest pod. A hostile process listening on a reused IP inside the 30-second window can still receive a request, which is a stated residual until R14's per-pod serving certificate lets the gateway verify which pod answered; this listener adopts that certificate when R14 lands. This is the only part of the design that waits for R14.
  - **Order.** The gateway inserts the record, pushes it as a binding change, and stamps the label, and it forwards the first request only after the stamp succeeds. A transient stamp failure returns a retry hint, and a reconciler retries the label, because `NewlyPinned` fires once and a failed stamp would otherwise wedge the pod. `pkg/binding` treats a record with no visible label as not yet visible and fails closed on a store read error or on a record that disagrees with the label, so a pod with a record is never evaluated under the warm-up policy. The decision service does not cache decisions for an unlabelled service-mode pod, so a warm-up decision cannot outlive the pin.
  - **Lifecycle and scope.** The record is removed when its pod's deletion is observed, and a periodic sweep removes records whose UID no longer exists. The table is platform-scoped, because a lookup by pod UID has no tenant context, and §12 declares it as such. §12.8 erasure retires the tenant's service-mode pods before it deletes their records, and §12.9 classifies them. A §25.11 restore can lose recent records, so the gateway rebuilds them from pod labels at start.
  - **Availability.** This adds Postgres to the service-mode request path for a new pin and for a replica's route miss. During a store outage, requests that an already loaded pin can serve continue, and any other request returns `WARM_POOL_EXHAUSTED` with a retry hint. During an API server outage, the 30-second snapshot limit stops routing on that replica, with the same answer. §5.2's in-memory statement changes accordingly.

`netagent` caches each minted credential until the earlier of its `expiresAt` and the I8 response's `cacheUntil`, and refreshes it shortly before, so a binding under `npm ci` or `gh` costs one I8 call per lifetime rather than one per request. The cache is keyed on the binding generation, the session (the tenant pin for `always` bindings), and the binding definition's version, and `netagent` flushes it on every I7 binding change and session end. A tenant admin's edit of a binding definition (a host removed, a binding deleted) bumps that version, and the gateway pushes it as a binding change, so the edit reaches the pod at once rather than at the credential's expiry, so a credential never outlives the session it was minted for on a recycled, service-mode, or concurrent-session pod. A stored secret has no provider expiry, so the Token Service assigns it a cache lifetime of 15 minutes, as its `cacheUntil`, which bounds how long a rotation takes to reach a pod. An absent `cacheUntil` means `expiresAt`. An I8 response also carries `handling`, a list of names with an optional minimum version, and `netagent` refuses a credential naming anything it does not implement at that version, so an older image cannot attach a credential without a required step. `handling` is empty until a proposal in another design sets it. Revision 5 had the decision service relay the pod's token to the Token Service; a compromised decision service would then have held a valid token for every active pod and could have replayed each for anything the pod's binding allowed. The direct path removes the relay.

**The I8 listener.** I8 is the first pod-reachable surface of the component that holds every provider credential, and today the Token Service admits only the gateway over mTLS (§13.2.4). I8 therefore runs on its own port and listener with its own connection and goroutine budget, so pod load cannot starve the gateway-facing lease path or trip the gateway's Token Service breaker (F-4.3.3); it caps connections and requests per source address before authentication, and per pod and per tenant after it; it serves TLS under the egress CA, which `netagent` verifies (see "Transport security"), so a stored push token or an API key never crosses the cluster network in cleartext; and P7 counts credential calls with the other quotas. The `proxied`, `restricted` with `gitAccess`, and `internet` with `netagent` policies gain the I8 port as a pod-side peer, and the `lenny-system` ingress rule for the Token Service gains the agent namespaces on that port only. The mirror's own fetches use the same listener on the mirror's mTLS identity, scoped to the store's tenant and upstream and naming the pool and the cause of the fetch.

**When the Token Service is unavailable**, `netagent` fails closed: a request to a bound host with no valid cached credential gets 503 from `netagent`, a federation token that cannot be rewritten before expiry is removed at expiry, and a `metadata` fetch returns 503. Cached credentials keep working until they expire, which bounds the outage a session notices to the credential lifetimes. The other consumers fail closed the same way: the gateway's `ls-refs` for a private repository returns `GIT_CLONE_REF_RESOLVE_TRANSIENT`, the adapter's materialization clone retries within its timeout, and a tenant-owned pool's warm fill retries with backoff while the pod stays out of READY, as 0111 requires, which the pool capacity alert surfaces.
- It receives `netagent`'s local decisions over I7 (path enforcement refusals, redirect refusals, scrub hits, and tunnels closed at a binding change) and records them with the central ones, each with a decision identifier that the refusal returned to the agent carries. `lenny-ctl` queries decisions by session, so a 403 is traceable whichever hop produced it.
- It serves I3 on a listener that the `lenny-system` NetworkPolicy admits only from `lenny-egress`, mirror, and Token Service pods, and from gateway pods at level 1, I7 on a separate port with a per-pod rate limit, so a pod cannot flood the decision path, and the binding push on a port admitted only from gateway pods.

**Port scoping.** Every pod-side egress rule names a port: the proxy port on `lenny-egress`, the I7 port on `lenny-egress-authz`, the minting port on the Token Service, the git port on the mirror, and 53 on the resolver, as the chart already does for every existing peer. Envoy's admin interface, whose endpoints (`/quitquitquit`, `/drain_listeners`, `/runtime_modify`, `/config_dump`) authenticate nothing, binds to loopback inside the `lenny-egress` pod, and its metrics are served on a separate listener that the `lenny-system` policy admits only from the scrape source. The access log service port on `lenny-egress-authz` admits only `lenny-egress`. A tier 9 test confirms that a pod cannot open any other port on any of these pods.

**Private destinations.** A destination on a private address (a corporate gateway, an internal GitLab, or any platform-registered private service) is never resolved by Envoy. The registration stores the address, the grant names the tenants, and the decision returns the address in dynamic metadata for a granted tenant, so an `ORIGINAL_DST` cluster dials it. A private origin (a GitLab or a CRM) is reached by terminating the CONNECT and tunnelling raw bytes to that cluster, whereas a private upstream proxy receives a forwarded CONNECT; the spike validated the forwarded form, and round 6 validated the raw-tunnel form, including stock git through a pinned address. At the default level and level 2 a pod therefore has no network path of its own to a private destination, so a raw socket from any pod is dropped, and the `ipBlock` entries belong to the central proxy alone. A level 2 proxy must dial the address the decision returns and never resolve the name itself. `ORIGINAL_DST` gives Envoy this behaviour, and `centralProxy.level2.pinnedDestinations` defaults to `false`, so a deployer asserts this capability, and validators refuse private destinations until it is `true`. A tenant route may name only upstreams, endpoints, and groups the tenant holds, and the decision refuses any other. A compromised decision service can steer a tenant to any registered `ipBlock`; that residual is bounded by the `lenny-egress` NetworkPolicy, which admits only the registered addresses. The address filter keeps every private range with no exemption, which closes the path where a tenant points a hostname it controls at another tenant's private service. A tenant-registered public service (an upstream proxy or a git endpoint) is resolved at registration, its addresses are stored, the registration is refused when any address falls in the deployment's full exclusion list, in a registered `ipBlock`, or in the cluster's own node and load-balancer addresses (a hairpin to the cluster's public address is public by range), and the decision service re-resolves it periodically and suspends the registration when a later answer fails the same check. Private registrations stay with the platform admin, because each needs an `ipBlock` in the `lenny-egress` NetworkPolicy.

### Evidence streams

The egress and git path produces records that three audiences use: the tenant admin debugging a refusal, the customer's security team, and the operator's forensics team. These records are specified here, because their format is costly to change later and this design is their first producer. Other designs consume them (see "Reserved for other designs").

**Model.** Evidence is a set of typed streams with one common envelope. A new kind of evidence is a new stream, with no change to any existing stream, to the store, or to export. Lenny does not extend an existing stream's payload with a different kind of evidence, because streams differ in volume, retention, sensitivity, and trust.

| Envelope field | Meaning |
| :-- | :-- |
| `stream` and `schemaVersion` | The stream's name and its payload version. Payloads change by adding fields or a new version. |
| `producer`, `incarnation`, and `sequence` | The verified producing service, taken from its SAN, a value that changes when an instance restarts and that also separates a service's replicas, and a sequence number per producer, incarnation, stream, and tenant, so a consumer of one tenant's records can detect a gap. An incarnation with no closing record may have lost its unflushed records. A producer that cannot number its own records, which is Envoy, has them numbered at ingest, and `incarnation` then names the writing replica's incarnation. |
| `observedAt` | When the producer observed the event. |
| `tenantId`, `poolId`, `podUid`, `sessionId`, `environment`, `crossedFromEnvironment` | Attribution, where the event has it, set from the verified binding and session row and never from a producer's claim. A warm pod's records have no tenant and sit under a platform prefix, as do a quarantined pod's records, so a tenant's reads do not reveal a quarantine. |
| `traceId` | The session's trace identifier, which joins records across streams and to tool calls. |
| `provenance` | `enforced` for a record from a component outside the pod that the action passed through, `observed` for one from a component outside the pod that watched without being in the path, and `self_reported` for one from inside a pod or from a component outside the platform's control, such as a customer's connector agent. A consumer treats a `self_reported` record as evidence only while its producer is uncompromised. |
| `payloadKeyRef` | Absent on these streams. On a later stream with sensitive content, it names a key for a session or a tenant and pod incarnation that encrypts the payload (see **Erasure and holds**). |
| `sequenceEnd` | Optional. A record can carry a run of records as one sequence range: a chunk of one session's payloads, because a payload encrypted per record does not compress in a batch (about 226 bytes per payload against about 40 in round 11), or an aggregate of repeated denials. A range covers contiguous sequence numbers only. On a stream encrypted per session the sequence is per session and the position includes the session, because two sessions interleaved in one tenant's sequence overlap, and one session's range would hide the other's lost chunk. |
| `payload` | The typed body. |

**Streams in this design:**

| Stream | Producer | Provenance | Payload |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `egress.decision` | The decision service | `enforced`, and `self_reported` at level 1, where no action passes through it | Decision identifier, authority, port, peer address, outcome, rule, route target, egress group, and any shadow policy's outcome |
| `egress.tunnel` | Envoy, through the access log stream | `enforced` | Decision identifier, upstream address, upstream source port, start, end, bytes in each direction, and how it ended |
| `egress.accesslog` | Envoy, directly to `lenny-evidence` under its own identity | `enforced` | The fields Envoy itself measures, with the decision identifier as a lookup key, so a reconciler has a source a compromised decision service does not control |
| `egress.local` | `netagent`, through I7 | `self_reported` | Path refusals, redirect refusals, scrub hits, tunnels closed at a binding change, each tunnel's local client port with its decision identifier, which joins a process to a tunnel, and at level 1 its tunnel opens and closes |
| `git.operation` | `netagent` through I7, and the mirror | `self_reported` from `netagent`, `enforced` from the mirror | Operation, repository, refs with old and new SHAs for a push, fetched commit, and the route used |
| `credential.mint` | The Token Service | `enforced` | Pod UID, binding and its definition version, provider, scope, hosts, expiry, and a token hash or JWT `jti` that joins to provider logs. Stored secrets record the secret's version. No value is recorded. |

Administrative writes to egress and git settings stay in the §11.7 audit log, because they are low-rate and already fit it. `credential.leased` keeps its current meaning. No stream records a URL's query string or path outside the fields named here. Authorities and redirect targets are attacker-chosen, so they are bounded in length and limited to host syntax before they are stored.
