**Ingest.** A single `lenny-evidence` Deployment is the only component that writes evidence, so no producer holds a store credential that a compromised mirror or Token Service could use against other tenants' records. It holds the bucket credential, the manifest database role, and any T4 KMS rights. Producers call it over mTLS under the egress PKI, and its client SAN allowlist binds each producer to the streams it may write. A later per-node producer authenticates with a projected token carrying pod and node claims instead, and its records must name a pod on its own node, so `pkg/binding`'s projection includes `spec.nodeName`. Ingest sets `producer` from the verified SAN and ignores what a record claims. The decision service forwards `netagent`'s reports only after overwriting tenant, pool, pod, and session from the verified token and binding, rejecting a report for a session the binding does not name, forcing `self_reported`, and namespacing its identifiers, so a compromised `netagent` cannot pose as another producer or tenant. Ingest limits the mirror's records to tenants whose routes name it. A compromised mirror parent can still forge `enforced` `git.operation` records for those tenants, which is a stated limit. Each producer has a NetworkPolicy row to this one service. Buffers are per tenant, and repeated denials aggregate to a count per host, so one tenant's flood cannot cause gaps for another (P7). An aggregate covers a contiguous sequence range and closes when another record falls between, so a loss between denials stays visible. A decision identifier encodes its producer, incarnation, and sequence, so looking up the identifier in a 403 during a flood resolves to the aggregate whose range holds it. Aggregation keeps the host, the count, and the range, and it drops each repeat's other fields. Contiguity costs compression: two alternating hosts reduced records by 8% in round 12, where one host reduced them by a factor of 67,000. A flood of distinct hosts defeats aggregation, and its records count against the flooding tenant's bound, past which they are lost with a gap record. Round 12 lost none at 3,000 per second and announced every loss at 100,000 per second.

**Store.** §11.7's hash chain serializes per tenant and is sized for about 20 writes per second, which these streams exceed. `lenny-evidence` therefore writes through an `EvidenceStore` interface, as the other §12 stores do. The default writes objects under `/{tenant_id}/evidence/{stream}/{hour}/` in the object store, with a batch manifest in Postgres holding the producer, sequence range, time range, and digest. Producers that number their own records hash tenants to ingest replicas, so one writer usually serves a tenant, and a producer rehashes to a live replica within seconds when one stops, so a restart does not outlast its buffer. Envoy's access log stream cannot hash per tenant, so `egress.accesslog` reaches every replica, and a tenant-hour of that stream has one writer per replica until compaction. Positions never assume one writer. A tenant's batch flushes at a size bound or 60 seconds, so an idle tenant writes nothing. A compactor merges each tenant-hour into one object per stream with one manifest row, which keeps the retained manifest at no more than 720,000 rows per day for 5,000 tenants across the six streams, and fewer for tenants that are idle. Before compaction a busy deployment inserts far more (round 11 measured 10,000 rows per minute at 2,500 tenants on three streams), and the object count follows, so the manifest is partitioned by hour and compaction runs continuously. A compacted row holds the digest of its ordered part list, and the list sits in the object's index, because a row holding the list measured 7.4 KB, or about 5 GB per day at the bound, kept for the retention period. It concatenates the original compressed batches byte for byte, with an offset index holding their ordered digests. Each original digest therefore still recomputes from the object and its index, and each compaction writes an anchor event holding the digest of what it merged. Each interval, an audit event naming the tenant's new batches and their digests goes into the §11.7 chain, so the chain anchors the streams without carrying them, and the compactor cannot drop or rewrite a batch unseen. The interval event has to name the batches: with a digest alone, a compromised compactor that dropped a batch and rewrote a consistent object, index, row, and anchor passed the verifier in round 12, and only the sequence audit showed the hole. Naming them measured about 1.2 to 1.5 KB per event at 40 tenants with 5 s of batches. Retention writes a chain event naming what it removed, because the verifier otherwise reports the removed batches as missing. Retention is per stream, in the §16 retention presets. Region and tenant key follow the tenant's `dataResidencyRegion` and T4 key, as other tenant objects do.

**Failure behaviour.** For most tenants a decision does not wait for evidence, because egress availability would otherwise depend on the object store. A producer buffers within a per-tenant bound, and past it the lost records appear as a sequence gap, a gap record, and an alert. A producer's crash loses what it buffered, and its next incarnation makes that detectable. An ingest replica's crash loses what that replica buffered, up to one flush interval, and it is not recoverable. The producer's incarnation does not change in that case, and a lost tail has no later record to reveal it, so ingest reports each tenant and stream's durable sequence to the producer after each flush, and a producer that loses a replica writes a gap record through another replica for the range between the last durable and the last sent sequence. A gap record states possible loss. A frozen replica can flush after its producer gave up on it, so a range can hold stored records, which take precedence, and a retried request can store a record twice, which a consumer removes by position. Each replica also writes a row for its own incarnation when it starts and when it closes, and it renews the row's lease at each flush. A row with an expired lease and no closing row marks a loss window, from its last flush less one flush interval until the lease expired, which is the only signal for `egress.accesslog`, since it has no producer sequence. The window covers every tenant that replica served, and it does not say which lost records. A tenant whose `complianceProfile` is a regulated value gets a stronger rule, because its record must exist before the connection does: the decision service sends the decision to `lenny-evidence` and waits for a group commit to a Postgres write-ahead table, draining to the store later, and it refuses the CONNECT with a 503 if the commit does not return within 150 ms, which sits below the chart's 250 ms floor for the ext_authz timeout. This is the pattern §11.2 uses for billing events. Each tenant commits in its own transaction under §12.3's tenant guard, because the sentinel that spans tenants is reserved for platform-admin paths. A refusal is a stated availability cost of this rule: a write-ahead log stall on the database's disk causes a burst of refusals (see **Measured**). The 503 carries a distinct reason, `netagent` retries that reason once after 200 ms plus up to 300 ms of jitter, and a metric and an alert report the refusal rate per tenant. The retry clears a refusal only when its delay outlasts the stall, and a 503 from Envoy itself has no reason and gets no retry. A refusal can follow a commit that landed late, so a stored allow without a tunnel record does not prove a connection, and the producer names each refused sequence it cannot settle in a gap record, since a replica that never received it cannot.

Envoy's access log stream is lossy, so `egress.tunnel` can lack an end time and bytes. A consumer treats such a record as open, and each `egress.decision` record exists independently of it. The chart's graceful drain is required for this stream as well as for tunnels. With the drain, 1,000 of 1,000 close records arrived. With a plain `SIGTERM`, 32 did. A crashed Envoy delivers none, and those tunnels end at the heartbeat rule under "Egress decision service".

**Reads and export.**

- A tenant reads its own streams through `GET /v1/egress/decisions` and a stream export, filtered by session, host, outcome, and time, with a summary of denied hosts. Each read needs a named permission (`evidence.read.<stream>`), which `tenant-admin` holds by default.
- A platform admin can query across tenants by egress address, source port, target, and time, which answers an abuse report. Source ports are reused, so all of those filters matter. On a shared address the query needs the cloud NAT log as well, so the operations documentation requires those logs to be retained. The query reads manifests by time and scans objects, and a cross-tenant scan over T4 tenants runs in each region under the platform's KMS rights. Round 11 scanned a 14.4 million record busy hour in 5 to 17 seconds compacted and 18.5 to 79 seconds uncompacted on local MinIO, and a cloud store's GET latency weighs most on the uncompacted case, which has 150,000 objects. An index can be added from the compacted objects later with no format change.
- Export is its own path, because §11.7's delivery state is per `audit_log` row. Each consumer holds a cursor of delivered ranges per stable position (producer, incarnation, stream, tenant, and sequence), which do not change when batches compact, and gap records sit on ranges of their own. A single high-water mark per position lost about 6,000 records in round 12, because a gap record moved it past records that landed later. An incarnation's position leaves the cursor once it is closed and fully delivered, because the cursor otherwise grows with each restart (10,000 positions took 738 KB in round 11), and records are translated to OCSF at delivery. `lenny-evidence` serves reads from a named position, and retention honours each registered consumer's position up to a configured bound, because §25.5's webhook has no replay. A tenant subscription uses §25.5, with denied decisions never sampled.

**Erasure and holds.** `egress.*`, `git.operation`, and `credential.mint` carry a session identifier and no user identifier, and they are the same class as §12.8's `audit_log`: `DeleteByUser` deletes nothing from them under the same tamper-evidence and legal-obligation basis, tenant deletion removes them, and §12.9 classifies them. A later stream with sensitive content encrypts each payload under a per-session key named by `payloadKeyRef`. Erasure destroys the key, pushes an invalidation to every ingest replica, and purges that session's buffered plaintext, which becomes a gap record with reason `erased_before_flush`. A cached copy also has a short lifetime, for a replica the push misses. In round 12, a replica that was not told wrote 40 of 140 later records under the destroyed key, and 300 buffered payloads stayed readable under the new key until the purge was added. A later record for that session gets a new key. The anchored digests, computed over ciphertext, still verify. Each manifest row has a held marker that retention, the compactor, `EvidenceStore.DeleteByTenant`, and key destruction honour, because destroying a held session's key made its held payload unreadable in round 12. Ingest refuses a deleted tenant's later records, because records arriving after `DeleteByTenant` recreated that tenant's rows. `forensics-evidence-preservation-design.md` sets the marker (see "Reserved for other designs"), and the interface has an operation that escrows marked batches and rewraps their keys.

**Later streams.** Feedback on Lenny asks for agent activity evidence, such as bash commands the agent runs and files it opens. That is a later `agent.activity` stream, or several, owned by `forensics-evidence-preservation-design.md`. The spec records nothing of this kind today. The model above is what keeps it additive. Its volume and sensitivity differ, because commands and paths can contain secrets, and its trust differs, because a producer inside the pod is `self_reported`, whereas a node-level sensor would be `observed`. So it gets its own stream, retention, permission, and key, and it joins these records through `traceId`, `podUid`, and `sessionId`.

**Measured.** Round 9 ran this with a stand-in on a laptop. At 2,000 CONNECTs per second across 200 tenants, asynchronous evidence added no measurable CONNECT latency (p50 0.6 ms against 0.5 ms, p99 4.2 ms against 5.1 ms), with 24,000 of 24,000 close records delivered and no sequence gap. Durable commit before allow added about 9 ms at p50, with a p99 of 35 ms, and no failures at 2,000 per second, which round 11 did not reproduce. Every allowed CONNECT had a committed row with Postgres frozen (3,022 allowed, 3,025 rows) and with the service killed (1,982 and 1,982). With the object store frozen and a small buffer, 13,109 records were dropped and exactly 13,109 appeared as missing sequence numbers. A flipped byte failed its digest, and a deleted manifest row failed its anchor. These records compress better than production ones would, because the targets repeat.

Round 11 ran `lenny-evidence` as a separate service with two replicas behind mTLS, Envoy 1.40.2, MinIO, Postgres 17, and PgBouncer in transaction mode, at 2,500 tenants with varied authorities, on a laptop. Every figure carries that limit.

| Check | Result |
| :-- | :-- |
| CONNECT latency at 2,000 per second | p50 0.54 ms off and 0.59 ms on, p99 1.4 ms off and 3.2 ms on with `egress.accesslog` |
| Cost at about 9,700 records per second | 120m to 128m and about 630 MiB per replica, 276m in the decision service, 206m in the object store, and 13m in Postgres |
| Commit bound, through the pooler | 19 of 89,864 refused at 500 per second, and 3,013 of 778,402 (0.39%) at 2,000 per second, in bursts of up to about 1,700 |
| Commit bound, one transaction per tenant | 0 of 44,933 at 500 per second, and 122 of 179,677 (0.07%) at 2,000 per second. Round 12 did not reproduce these: 331 of about 90,000 (0.37%) and 1,309 of about 360,000 (0.36%), with one run at 0.74%. Runs differ by more than the modes do. |
| Allowed against stored | Allowed never exceeded stored, with the pooler paused (42,504 and 43,982) and with a replica killed (37,122 and 37,122) |
| Noisy tenant | Quiet tenants had 0 gaps in every case, with p99 CONNECT latency of 1.9 to 3.7 ms beside a flood |
| Replica killed | Between 826 and 2,425 tail records were invisible to a consumer, and `egress.accesslog` lost about 80,000 with no gap. Round 12 ran the signals the text adds: every lost tail and hole was announced after a kill, with one gap record per position with a tail. |
| Compaction | 300,000 batches merged into 5,000 rows in 65 s, and every digest, chain link, and anchor verified. A flipped byte and a dropped batch were both detected. |
| Export across compaction | 1,919,780 of 1,919,780 delivered, with 0 duplicates. An earlier run left 1 of 3,167,692 undelivered, and its cause was not established. |

The refusals did not come from the pooler, the drain, or ingest CPU, since each was excluded by its own run. `fdatasync` stalled up to 124 ms beside the load and the wait events at refusal times were `WalSync` and `WALWrite`, so this laptop's disk is the likely cause. A production disk will differ, and this run cannot say in which direction. The rule's refusal rate on a production database is still owed.

Round 12 also measured the following, on the same setup with prototypes written to revision 42. Two decision replicas wrote 1,439,489 records with 0 gaps. A rehash on replica loss dropped 0 records, against 37,843 at 40 s without it. A retry at 200 ms plus jitter left 12 visible refusals of 4,905 during 0.4 s stalls, and 24,004 of 31,755 during 2 s stalls, adding load equal to the refusals (2% and 13%) and raising late-landing commits. `egress.local`, `git.operation`, and `credential.mint` beside 2,000 CONNECTs per second raised p99 from 2.04 to 3.07 ms and p99.9 from 5.1 to 14.6 ms. A compacted row with its list in the index measured 392 B against 7,608 B, or about 282 MB per day at the bound against 5.5 GB by extrapolation, and a no-op export pass then costs one read per row, which is about 12 to 24 minutes at the bound for one worker. Reads from a named position, retention by consumer position, and holds under compaction, retention, and `DeleteByTenant` worked. Cause was not established for 1 of 3,167,692 records left undelivered in round 11.

### Git on covered hosts (`netagent`)

Git keeps its remote URLs. The pod's git config sets `http.proxy` to the local egress endpoint, so `git remote -v` prints the upstream URL, and `gh` and `glab` read the upstream host from it. `netagent` has one local listener. The config is under "Git configuration".

**A host is VCS-covered, per tenant, when `netagent` terminates it for git.** The covered hosts of a bound pod are the hosts of every VCS pool prefix and every git route of the tenant (to the mirror, a git endpoint, or `direct`), read unfiltered by environment and across `environmentPolicies`, so a session in E2 on a host only E1's pool names gets a refused push rather than a tunnel, plus the provider alias hosts shipped host data lists for those pools, such as `gist.github.com`, and `*.visualstudio.com` beside `dev.azure.com`. A warm pod with no tenant has the `gitRepository` hosts of the Runtime's `sharedAssets` and the pool's warm-up policy, and after bind those hosts count only if the tenant's own pools or routes name them, with one exception: on a `restricted` pool with `gitAccess`, every host of the formula counts as covered for that pool's pods, so a push there is refused and recorded rather than tunnelled. A provider's declared LFS transfer hosts are covered with it. GitHub's store host is an allowed host rather than a declared transfer host, and on a `restricted` pool it terminates and forwards with nothing attached. The formula rule is **[design]**: a host held as data ran, with a push refused and recorded, and the computed formula has not. Matching uses the shared canonicalizer on the host name, whatever the port. `netagent` receives the set on its I7 binding subscription and terminates each host with the per-pod CA (see "Delivery modes"), whose name constraints list them where the anchor carries constraints.

**`netagent` classifies smart HTTP on every terminated host,** whether covered or named by an `inject` binding. A `file` binding terminates nothing, so its host stays opaque with no pool or route. On a terminated connection, or in an absolute-form plain HTTP request, a smart HTTP or LFS path takes the git path below, whatever credential the client sent, and `netagent` removes that credential. On a host with no VCS pool, a fetch carries no credential, even with one from the client, and a push, its advert, and an LFS upload are refused **[measured]**. A tenant that wants pushes there adds a pool, and a stored token needs the `storedTokenPush` grant, so a binding's secret never attaches to git. Any other request on the host takes `inject` when a binding names the host, and is otherwise forwarded unchanged with nothing attached.

**The git path's fail-closed rules.** A prototype holds each of these, and 139 of 139 rows matched, on a covered host, a bound host with no pool, a bound host with rules, and an API host, including HTTP/2 and absolute-form spellings **[measured]**:

- **Classification runs before any other filter,** on the path decoded twice and case-folded. A path holding `info/refs`, `git-upload-pack`, `git-receive-pack`, `info/lfs`, `.git/`, or a provider's declared LFS transfer path is a git request.
- **A marker match that is not a canonical git path is refused.** The canonical form is the repository path, an optional `.git`, and the service or LFS path, with no `%`, doubled slash, dot segment, `;`, trailing slash, or suffix. So `git-receive-pack/`, `.json`, `//`, and `%2d` spellings never reach `inject` or the origin. **The cost is false positives on API hosts,** because a marker match is a substring: `GET .../contents/.git/config`, `.../contents/docs/info/refs`, and `.../contents/git-upload-pack.md` are refused **[measured]**, and so would be a repository named `git-receive-pack`, an Artifactory `info/lfs` repository, and `go get` on a `.git/` module path. No command in the matrix sent such a path.
- **Query.** `info/refs` takes exactly one `service=`, and the value must be `git-upload-pack` or `git-receive-pack`.
- **Overrides.** All 13 headers on the request rules' list are refused on the git path, and `netagent` forwards only the headers git and git-lfs send, so `X-Evil`, `Cookie`, `Private-Token`, and `X-Api-Key` do not reach the host and `Git-Protocol` does **[measured]**. The routing override list, `X-Original-URL`, `X-Rewrite-URL`, `X-Forwarded-Path`, `X-Forwarded-Prefix`, `X-Forwarded-Uri`, and `X-Envoy-Original-Path`, is refused on every terminated host, with or without rules, because a binding's secret staying off git otherwise depends on the origin ignoring those headers **[measured]**. A method override is on the longer list alone, so on a terminated host with no rules it is forwarded.
- **`git-receive-pack` bodies.** A compressed body gets 415 before its commands are read, and a `push-cert` is refused, because `netagent` reads the ref commands and enforces patterns on them.

**On a host no pool, route, or binding names, git goes through an opaque tunnel.** `netagent` sees a CONNECT authority alone, so none of the bullets below applies, and a token the agent holds reaches the host. "Residual risks and limits", **Uncovered git hosts**, states the loss and what the design gives instead.

- **Fetch** goes to the git endpoint that the tenant's git routes name for the host: `lenny-git-mirror`, a deployer git service, or the upstream host through `lenny-egress`. A deployer git service on a private address is reached through `lenny-egress`, as a raw tunnel to the address the decision pins. One rule overrides the route: a repository under a VCS pool whose provider is not `mint-scoped` (`mint-coarse` or `stored`) is never served through the mirror, because the mirror may hold only tokens the host itself limits to one repository. `netagent`, the gateway resolver, and the mirror apply the same rule from the pool's provider level, so no component needs to know whether a repository is private before a 401: such a repository goes `direct` through `lenny-egress`, the resolver resolves it upstream, and the mirror refuses it. A tenant-owned pool's `gitRepository` warm fill on such a provider fills from the pod through `netagent`. For private repositories, `netagent` attaches the endpoint's credential (its service account token for the mirror, or a read token from I8 for a deployer service or the upstream). On a pool with `maxConcurrentSessions > 1`, the readable and writable sets are the union over the pod's sessions, because `netagent` cannot attribute a request to a slot (§13.1 **Per-slot credential-read scope**). The tenant accepts that with `acknowledgeProcessLevelIsolation`, and the documentation states that any slot can push to any repository in the union.
- **Push** requests a write token from the Token Service (I8) for the repository in the request path. The Token Service checks the session's write scope and the tenant's repository allowlist, with the decision service's scope decision, before it mints the token. `netagent` forwards the push through `lenny-egress` with the token, which stays in `netagent` memory (P3).
- **A credential the client sends** on the git path is dropped, and the mint is used. An `http.extraHeader`, a URL token, a credential helper, and `gh auth git-credential` all ended this way on a covered host, with patterns holding **[measured]**. **The one exception is a session that names a `file` binding under `allowRuntimeHeldCredentials`, which UC-5 owns. `netagent` attaches the current value it holds for that host on every git request, whatever the client sent, in the form the mint uses, and no mint goes to `netagent` for that host, and the Token Service refuses one.** This ran with all conditions holding **[measured]**. With no current value, as when the binding is suspended or unreleased, nothing attaches and the host answers 401 **[measured]**. `netagent` asks I8 first and a Token Service outage gives 503 **[design]**, since the run held its state in a file and made 0 Token Service requests. A read on a mirror route comes from the mirror's App mint, because the mirror's own I8 request is not refused, and a rebuild's read mint in the provisional state is not refused either, because it grants no write token. An image without this refuses the release, through a `handling` value **[measured]**, on a Token Service stand-in. After a 401 on a covered host git asked twice, and the origin saw only the mint **[measured]**.
- **Read after push**: after a successful push through the mirror route, `netagent` asks the mirror to refresh that repository's current store generation and holds the next fetch of the same repository from the same pod until the refresh completes, so a stale mirror cannot rewind `origin/*`. The mirror accepts a refresh request only for a repository in the caller's binding, and rate-limits it per repository. The spike did not implement or run this, so it is owed.
- **Path enforcement**: `netagent` refuses repositories outside the binding's readable or writable sets, whatever a token's scope. On a covered host with no VCS pool, a fetch of a public repository passes with no credential, and a push is refused.
- **Redirects**: `netagent` passes an in-scope redirect (same provider, HTTPS, allowlisted repository) to git, whose next request comes back through the proxy and is terminated in turn, and it refuses every other redirect with 403. An in-scope redirect was followed and an out-of-scope one got 403 **[measured]**. Neither GitHub nor GitLab redirects renamed repositories on smart HTTP, so redirects are rare in practice.
- **LFS**: git-lfs derives its batch URL from the remote, so batch requests reach `netagent` on the terminated host, which forwards them to the host's LFS API. `netagent` reads the `operation` field of the batch request body: `upload` is a write, which takes the write-scope path (a write token and path enforcement), and `download` is a read. **`netagent` parses each batch response, and an unparseable one is refused. From each action, including under `_links`, it removes `Authorization`, `Private-Token`, and every header name on the shipped credential list, compared case-insensitively, and holds the values in memory.** The prototype removed `Authorization` alone, so the wider list is **[design]**. It attaches a held value only on a transfer matching the live action's method, host, repository, and path, before its `expires_in`, and any other transfer carries nothing, because an action can otherwise hand the agent a credential. GitLab's upload and proxied-download actions set `Authorization`, from a `Gitlab::LfsToken` valid for 7,200 s for a user actor, or by echoing the attached token for a deploy token **[source]**. Each provider's transfer paths and hosts are data in the host set, and a transfer on one of them takes the git path, such as GitLab's `<repo>.git/gitlab-lfs/objects/<oid>` on the covered host. In that form, with git-lfs 3.6.0 on a stub, a push and a smudged clone passed, the value reached only the matching request, verbose client output held it 0 times, and another object, method, or repository and an expired action attached nothing **[measured]**.
  - **An action on another host passes unchanged when it carries no credential.** That is GitHub's download form: one anonymous `POST` to `github.com/onnx/models.git/info/lfs/objects/batch` returned an action on `github-cloud.githubusercontent.com`, with a signed query and no `header` key **[live]**, so a transfer goes through the egress proxy as an opaque tunnel, those domains belong to the provider's host set and need public roots in git's trust, and the signed query reaches the client, limited to that object. An upload, a request with a token, and a transfer have not been read, and they are owed.
  - **`netagent` does not leave an action that cannot be delivered.** With a credential removed from an action on a host or path it does not treat as a transfer, an earlier run had git-lfs retry without exiting, at up to 28,896 `PUT`s in 8 s on one machine. So `netagent` replaces that object's `actions` with the batch API's per-object `error`, with code 422 and a message naming the host. git-lfs 3.6.0 then stopped with no transfers **[measured]**. A push exits 1 in under 0.1 s, printing the host and the code. A clone exits 128 in 0.1 s with `smudge filter lfs failed` and `Clone succeeded, but checkout failed`, and **its terminal output does not name the host**, so the runtime author guide points to `git lfs logs last`, which was not read. Codes 404 and 500 behaved the same.
  - **The failure refusal stays, with a narrow job.** The per-object `error` acts at batch time, and it cannot cover a deliverable transfer the store then refuses on a terminated path. There `netagent` answers 403 after 3 consecutive failures on an object, until a new batch response. On a 401 this capped transfers at 3 per batch **[measured]**: 27 at the stub on a push over 35.9 s and 54 on a clone over 71.9 s. Each batch resets it, and the client exits on its own limit of 9 batches rather than on the refusal, which git-lfs treats as retryable. On a 500 it never fires, because git-lfs sends one transfer per batch. A terminated path with no such rule did not run, so the tight retry it guards against is inferred from the 28,896 figure on another path, and that inference is why it stays.
  - **`info/lfs` is refused on `mint-coarse` and `stored` providers until a live GitLab run passes,** which is owed, and `git push` of an LFS object fails there **[measured]**. The one live GitLab object tried returned an error rather than an action **[live]**. LFS objects are not mirrored.
- **Scrub**: the scrub under "Delivery modes" applies on the git path, to the minted or held value in response headers and bodies. A reflected held token was masked in a body and a header **[measured]**.
- **Streaming**: under this path, clone, fetch, shallow, partial, and 64 MiB default and chunked pushes passed on OpenSSL and GnuTLS git, with `memory.peak` at 28.7 to 29.1 MB **[measured]**.

**Git's trust.** Git uses the same bundle as every other tool: `/run/netagent/ca-bundle.pem`, holding the chart's public root set, any deployer CA, and the per-pod CA. `http.sslCAInfo` and `GIT_SSL_CAINFO` both name it, so the override `GIT_SSL_CAINFO` has over `http.sslCAInfo` changes nothing. The per-pod CA alone fails, because LFS storage hosts and submodules on an uncovered host are opaque tunnels carrying the host's own certificate. With it alone LFS and an unbound-host submodule failed on both builds, and with the bundle they passed **[measured]**. The cost is public roots in git's trust, and that a gap in coverage is silent, because git then succeeds through an opaque tunnel on the host's own certificate. The upstream check on the git path is the one `inject` states, with a registered endpoint's or outbound host's CA, so a covered host behind a tenant's intercepting gateway needs that registration, while an opaque host does not.

**Keeping coverage current.** A gap in the set must refuse rather than tunnel:

- The decision service refuses a pod's CONNECT to a covered host unless `netagent` sets the terminated marker, as "Credential-pinned hosts" describes, so a `netagent` with stale data gets 403 **[measured]**, on a stand-in. EG-3 ships this, because T does, and the cache key includes the marker.
- The adapter waits for the session's generation before materialization, so a clone does not run on stale data. In SDK-warm and service mode, where a process is already running, the first request to the pod waits the same way.
- A VCS pool, egress route, git route, platform default, target (upstream, endpoint, or outbound host), CA, or asset write pushes a binding change like a binding definition edit, a route write closes pooled upstream connections to the hosts it changes, and `netagent` closes open opaque tunnels to a newly covered, pinned, or read-only host.
- The generation wait and the clone hold draw on one fetch retry window, and on the resume path both are limited to 30 s, so they sit below their watchdogs and fail with `network_not_ready` or `git_fetch_pending` as the cause.

**Warm pods and CA coverage.** A host must be in `netagent`'s data and in the CA's name constraints before a warm fill. A pod with no tenant fills a warm-up asset through the mirror's public store when the deployment runs the mirror, and otherwise goes `direct`. A warm fill and a mirror route worked once it was **[measured]**. Before a binding, with the bundle, a host not yet covered reaches public repositories as an opaque tunnel **[measured]**. After a re-issue, the next run of each client passed with no restart **[measured]**, because git reads the bundle on each invocation.

**Other git clients.** go-git with defaults and JGit with the JVM proxy and truststore clone. libgit2 needs its proxy option, and with its default it fails on DNS **[measured]**. Two limits are stated in the runtime author guide:

- **libgit2 hangs on a constraint mismatch.** Where git, `gh`, and go-git return an error for a host outside the CA's constraints, a libgit2 client sat in a poll for 8 h 44 min in one run, and a rerun killed it at 45 s **[measured]**. The cause inside libgit2 was not diagnosed. A runtime using it sets a timeout of its own.
- **JGit does not enforce constraints.** The JVM's PKIX ignores a trust anchor's extensions, so JGit accepted a host outside the constraints **[measured]**. This is the limit "Delivery modes" states for JVM clients.

**Cost.** `netagent` used 689 to 714 ms of CPU per measured block against 520 to 541 ms on the prior path, about 35% more, and ten `ls-remote` took 115 to 116 ms against 87 to 101 ms **[measured]**. A 64 MiB clone and push took about the same.

The git path shares the `netagent` container with the egress endpoint, because they are one listener.

### Git mirror (`lenny-git-mirror`)

`lenny-git-mirror` is a Lenny-built StatefulSet with persistent volumes in `lenny-system`. It is a thin Go server over stock git, because no maintained, multi-tenant-aware git caching proxy exists to adopt (the first spike surveyed Goblet, git-cache-http-server, ngitcached, FINOS git-proxy, Artifactory, Nexus, and the hosting products' replicas).

- It serves upload-pack by running `git upload-pack --stateless-rpc` directly and flushing after each write. The second spike showed that Go's `net/http/cgi` holds keepalive and progress output until the response ends, and it rejects chunked request bodies.
- It keeps one bare repository per authorization boundary. A store's identity is the upstream URL, the fetch route, and the credential used. A public store is one filled anonymously through the platform default route, and it is the only store shared across tenants. A repository fetched through any tenant route, tenant-registered endpoint, or tenant credential gets a store keyed by that tenant as well, so a tenant cannot fill the store another tenant's warm fill reads. Objects never cross stores, because protocol v2 serves any object that exists by SHA without a reachability check.
- A public store whose upstream fetch starts failing with an authentication or not-found error (a repository made private or deleted) stops serving after one failed refresh, rather than serving its last copy to every tenant. Tenants that still need it fetch through their own credential into their own store.
- It authorizes each request twice: it resolves the caller's tenant through its own `pkg/binding` instance (the pod's label, or the gateway's asserted tenant) and refuses a store outside that tenant, and it asks I3 with the caller's service account token as the second gate, so a compromised decision service cannot serve one tenant's store to another. That I3 check names the repository, and the decision reads that readable entry's state, which is the path with no mint. The mirror therefore runs the same informer and holds the same RBAC and API server egress as the other two instances, which proposal 5 carries.
- It runs every `git fetch` and `upload-pack` in a sandboxed subprocess, because a hostile pack exploiting a git parsing defect would otherwise reach every tenant's stores and credentials. The sandbox bounds a compromised subprocess to one store:
  - **Capability posture.** Every `lenny-system` workload drops all capabilities and runs non-root (§13.1), and switching UIDs needs capabilities. Kubernetes writes `capabilities.add` to the bounding and permitted sets and never to the ambient set, so a non-root entrypoint holds nothing after `exec` (kubernetes/kubernetes#56374), and file capabilities and setuid bits are void under `allowPrivilegeEscalation: false`. The mirror container is therefore the one stated exception to both rules: its entrypoint starts as root with every capability dropped except `CAP_SETUID`, `CAP_SETGID`, and `CAP_CHOWN`, and before it does anything else it sets `PR_SET_KEEPCAPS`, switches to the parent UID (65532) with its supplementary groups kept (so the parent keeps the `fsGroup` that reads the 0440 Secret), restores the three capabilities to its effective and inheritable sets, and then raises them into the ambient set, in that order, because the kernel clears the ambient set on a root-to-non-root switch; `setpriv` implements the same sequence. The long-running parent is then non-root and keeps the three capabilities across its own `exec`s. `allowPrivilegeEscalation: false` and the read-only root filesystem stay. The parent launches git through `setpriv` or an equivalent that sets the fetch UID and its one store group and clears the inheritable and ambient sets, because ambient capabilities survive a switch between non-root UIDs. Round 10 found that Go's `exec.Cmd` with `Credential` alone leaves the child all three capabilities and able to `setuid(0)`, so it is not an equivalent; a re-exec shim on one locked thread and a fork from a locked thread are. A thread with cleared capabilities must not return to Go's scheduler, or a later `Setgroups` aborts the process. The child then holds no permitted, effective, inheritable, or ambient capability. Its bounding set keeps the three capabilities, because clearing it needs `CAP_SETPCAP`, and `no_new_privs` with an empty ambient set leaves it no way to regain them. RuntimeDefault seccomp already permits `setuid`, `setgid`, `setgroups`, and `chown`, so no custom profile is needed. The exception needs the `lenny-system` namespace at the `baseline` Pod Security level or the mirror in its own `lenny-mirror` namespace labelled `baseline`, which the chart documents and preflight checks; preflight's posture check also extends to StatefulSets, which it skips today, and it reads namespace labels only, so a cluster-wide enforced default that the labels do not show is documented as a gap. §13.1 and §17 gain a row for the exception. Round 8 ran this sequence on Kubernetes 1.35 with `setpriv`, and a child at a fetch UID could write its own store and could not write or list another, read the mirror's Secret, write the root filesystem, signal the parent, become root, or switch UID. The sweep's `kill(-1)` from a helper under the fetch UID ended every process under that UID, including `setsid` and double-forked ones, and left the parent alone. The parent's re-chown did not follow a planted symlink to the Secret. The exception's cost is what a compromised parent can do: it can become UID 0 in the container, still limited to the three capabilities, so it could not mount, write sysctls, or read pid 1's environment, and it can write the volume as root. With `hostUsers` unset that UID 0 is the node's UID 0, which matters only if a kernel flaw also lets the process leave the container.

    User namespaces (`hostUsers: false`, stable in Kubernetes 1.36) reduce the risk of this exception without removing it. Pod Security stops constraining `runAsNonRoot` and `runAsUser` for such a pod, so the root entrypoint no longer needs an exception at that level, and a process that escapes the container is an unprivileged UID on the node. The capability rules do not relax: `restricted` still refuses `CAP_SETUID`, `CAP_SETGID`, and `CAP_CHOWN`, so the `baseline` prerequisite and the §13.1 drop-all exception stay. Adoption also needs Linux 6.3 or later, containerd 2.0 or CRI-O 1.25 or later, runc 1.2 or crun 1.9 or later, and idmap mount support on every volume filesystem, which excludes NFS, and the chart's floor is Kubernetes 1.27 today. A user-namespace pod's IDs default to the range 0 to 65535, which also has to hold the fetch UIDs and the store GIDs, which the ID rule under **Per-store ownership** keeps in range. A wider range is the kubelet's `userNamespaces.idsPerPod`, which is node configuration the chart does not control. Round 8 confirmed each of these points. With `hostUsers: false`, UID 0 in the container mapped to an unprivileged node UID in a range of 65,536. A fetch UID or store GID above that range failed with `EINVAL`, so no fetch ran. In range, every boundary above held as it did with the field unset, on a volume whose on-disk owners matched the IDs in the pod. With no added capabilities the switch failed with `EPERM`, and `restricted` still refused the three capabilities while admitting root with none. On Kubernetes 1.31.4, where the feature gate is off by default, the API server dropped the field with no error and the pod ran on the node's user namespace, so the setting needs a check in the running pod.

    **`gitMirror.hostUsers`** defaults to `true`, the Kubernetes default, and a deployer on suitable nodes can set it to `false`. With `false`, the mirror reads `/proc/self/uid_map` at start and refuses to start if UID 0 maps to node UID 0, so a cluster that dropped the field fails visibly and does not run with less protection than the operator believes. The default can change once the supported Kubernetes floor and node requirements allow it.
  - **Per-store ownership.** Every store, public or tenant-keyed, has its own GID from a range the mirror owns, recorded in the store index. Store GIDs and fetch UIDs stay below 65536 in every mode, because GIDs are written to disk and a user-namespace pod can map only that range by default, so changing `hostUsers` later needs no re-chown of the stores. A fetch runs under a per-fetch UID with exactly its store's GID as its only group, and the store directory is group-writable (`2770` for a tenant store, `2775` for a public store, which is public content), so a compromised fetch can write one store and no other, which is what closes the shared public store to poisoning through a hostile public repository. Each store sets `core.sharedRepository=group`, so the directories and packs a fetch creates are group-writable and a later fetch under another UID can write them, and after each fetch the parent re-chowns the store's contents to the store's owner UID, which never executes anything, so no fetch UID keeps ownership of files once it exits. That needs a volume honouring the parent's `CAP_CHOWN`. NFS does not: the server authorizes by the AUTH_SYS UID, and `lchown` got `EPERM` on 3 of 3 entries in round 12. The mirror therefore checks chown and default ACLs on its volume at start and does not become ready if chown fails, so NFS is refused as a mirror volume, and POSIX ACL calls also fail there. The re-chown walks the tree through directory file descriptors with `lchown`, never following a symlink a fetch may have planted, and it changes the owner only, so no group or mode change can be steered into another store; the parent takes the store's GID for the walk, because `CAP_CHOWN` grants no directory traversal. Every git invocation runs with a `safe.directory` setting, because after the re-chown neither a fetch UID nor a reader owns the store, and git otherwise refuses with `detected dubious ownership`. After the sweep, a helper under the fetch UID normalizes what the fetch wrote, setting modes and the store's ACL, restoring `config` from the store index and emptying `hooks`, and removing setuid and setgid bits, other ACL entries, `user.*` extended attributes, symlinks, and special files (a FIFO at `packed-refs` hung every later git in round 12). It keeps hard links, because removing them deleted `HEAD` in 4 of 4 runs, and the chart requires `fs.protected_hardlinks=1`, which stops links across stores. It does this because the parent has no `CAP_FOWNER`, a chown of a setuid file fails with `EPERM`, and a planted 0777 directory or 0666 file would otherwise let a later reader or another store's fetch write it. The sweep runs at fetch exit, before the re-chown and before the fetch counts as complete: the parent, which is a child subreaper (`PR_SET_CHILD_SUBREAPER`) so that orphaned descendants reparent to it, forks a helper that switches to the fetch UID and sends `kill(-1)` while the parent reaps orphans by pid, because `kill(-1)` keeps succeeding against zombies and never reaches `ESRCH` otherwise, and it then waits for every process under that UID to exit. The parent holds no `CAP_KILL`, and the helper needs none, because a process may signal processes of its own UID. A descendant a compromised fetch left behind therefore cannot keep writing the store after the fetch is reported done, and cannot inherit a later fetch's forwarder. A fetch UID returns to the free pool only after the sweep, once the parent has confirmed from `/proc` that no process remains under it, and the re-chown has completed. A UID whose sweep cannot be confirmed stays out of the pool. A container restart ends every process in the container, so the parent starts with an empty pool in use and re-chowns any store whose fetch was in flight, which the store index records. The range also bounds concurrent fetches, and a fetch waits when no UID is free. The re-chown is best-effort for a directory a fetch made unreadable to its group, which the sweep makes harmless. `upload-pack` for a public store runs with no group and reads through the world-readable bits, so a hostile client cannot write a public store. `upload-pack` for a tenant store runs with a per-store reader group that a POSIX default ACL grants read and search only, so a client-side exploit by the tenant's own agent cannot poison the tenant's store either; on a volume without ACL support the parent reports it and an alert fires, so the fallback is not silent, and it runs with the store's group, and the residual is a same-tenant poisoning the documentation states. The parent sets a store's mode and ACL before its chown, because it cannot afterwards. The mirror pod keeps the `fsGroup` the chart gives every `lenny-system` workload with `fsGroupChangePolicy: OnRootMismatch`, so the kubelet leaves the volume's ownership alone once the parent has initialized it on first start, and the mounted Secret that carries the I8 client key stays readable by the parent through the group at mode 0440 while fetch UIDs, whose only group is their store's, cannot read it.
  - **Identity and credentials.** The mirror's identity material (its service account tokens and its I8 client certificate) is readable by the parent UID only. The credential forwarder is per fetch, a Unix socket in the fetch's working directory whose parent end accepts only the fetch's UID (`SO_PEERCRED`) and pins the upstream host and repository path as well as the credential, so a subprocess cannot borrow a concurrent fetch's token or send its own token elsewhere.
  - **Network.** The subprocess shares the pod's network namespace, so network egress is not a sandbox property; what bounds it is that the subprocess holds no credential and no identity, that I3 and I8 require a client certificate only the parent can read, and that the `lenny-system` policy allows the mirror pod to reach only `lenny-egress`, I3, I8, the API server, and the pooler. At level 1 the mirror also holds `ipBlock` rows for private git endpoints, so a subprocess under a hostile pack can open raw connections to every registered private git endpoint, across tenants, with no credential (see "Level 1 replacement").
  - A tier 9 test runs a compromised-subprocess stand-in against each boundary. Tenant-keyed stores can be sharded onto separate StatefulSet replicas when a deployment wants a harder boundary.
- It fetches upstream through `lenny-egress` on its own `lenny-egress` audience token (the mirror's service account in `lenny-system`) with an `x-lenny-tenant` header naming the store's tenant. The decision service honours a tenant assertion only from the mirror, gateway, and Token Service identities, which it recognizes from the token's service account claim and checks for liveness through its `lenny-system` informer, and it then applies that tenant's routes (a private git endpoint or an egress group). Read credentials come from I8 on the mirror's client certificate, with tenant, upstream, pool, and the cause of the fetch (a session, a prefetch, or a warm fill) as the request: the Token Service checks that the tenant's VCS pool covers the upstream and that the tenant's git route names the mirror for that host, and releases `mint-scoped` read tokens only, so a compromised mirror parent cannot read the repositories of a tenant that never routed through it. `mint-coarse` tokens (an Azure DevOps Entra token is the identity's full token, with write capability) and `stored` credentials are never released to the mirror, because the Token Service cannot verify a mirror's tenant claim beyond the pool match, so repositories on those providers route `direct` from the pod under the rule stated under "Git on covered hosts". The mirror's parent process holds the credential and attaches it on a loopback forwarder that the sandboxed git subprocess fetches through, so the subprocess never holds it. A public store fetches anonymously through the platform default route. It serializes fetches per repository, sets `transfer.fsckObjects=true`, disables `gc.auto`, schedules bitmap repacks, and caps `pack.threads` and `pack.windowMemory` per request.
- It purges a tenant's stores under the §12.8 erasure flow (`DeleteByTenant`), and §12.9 classifies mirror contents and credential bindings.
- It serves the warm fill from proposal 0111 over smart HTTP.

The mirror is optional. These capabilities depend on it:

| Capability                      | With the mirror                                            | Without the mirror                                                                      |
| :------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Warm fill of large repositories | Pods fill from a local copy at LAN speed.                  | Each warm pod clones from the upstream host.                                            |
| Upstream load and rate limits   | One upstream fetch serves every pod.                       | Every pod fetch counts against the host's limits, and private fetches mint read tokens. |
| Upstream outage                 | The mirror resolves refs within its age limit and serves the objects it holds. | Clones fail until the host recovers.                                                    |
| Read credentials                | Held centrally in the mirror, for `mint-scoped` providers. | `netagent` requests a read token for each private fetch.                                |
| Consistency across pods         | All pods see the same refs until the next refresh.         | Each pod sees the upstream at its own fetch time.                                       |
| Validation                      | Every fetched object passes `fsckObjects` once, centrally. | Each pod relies on its own `transfer.fsckObjects`.                                      |

Without the mirror, git still works through `netagent` and `lenny-egress`, more slowly and with more upstream traffic.

## Credentials

### Current state

- A tenant admin creates credential pools. Since proposal 0112, each credential's Secret lives in the `lenny-credentials` namespace, and the Token Service reads it on each request.
- A VCS credential pool has a provider (today `github`), `hostPatterns` naming the git hosts it serves, and credentials. A tenant has at most one pool per host (`ResolveVCSPool`).
- The `github` provider's Secret holds a single stored `token`. §4.9 describes minting GitHub App installation tokens, but no code mints anything (F-4.9.44).
- In-pod git uses `git-credential-lenny`, which calls the `lenny/vcs_token` tool through the adapter. The tool returns the pool's stored token for any matching host. It ignores the requested mode and never checks the session's plan or the repository.
- The gateway's own clone (`gitClone`) sends the stored token as an HTTP header and never shows it to the pod. That path is broken in deployed images, which have no `git` binary (F-14.1.37).

### Target state

**Minting.** The Token Service mints short-lived tokens from long-lived provider credentials that never leave the `lenny-credentials` namespace.

- For `github` and GitHub Enterprise Server, the Secret holds `appId`, `privateKey`, and `installationId`. The Token Service signs an App JWT and calls `POST /app/installations/{id}/access_tokens` with `repositories` and a `permissions` subset (for example `contents: write`, and `pull_requests: write` for a credential binding). Tokens last one hour.
- GitHub documents caching installation tokens until they expire. The Token Service caches one token per (tenant, host, installation, sorted repository set, permissions). The cache is shared across its replicas and holds each token encrypted under the Token Service's envelope key, because a cache per replica would multiply mints by the replica count. It refreshes a token only when a caller asks for it within a margin of expiry, with one mint at a time per key, so a key nobody is using costs nothing. One mint serves every session with the same key, so a deployment mints about once per hour per key in use: far fewer than one per session where sessions share repositories, and several per session where they do not, because a clone, a push, and an API binding each hold a different key. GitHub's documented secondary limits that bound minting are 900 points per minute per REST endpoint, where a POST costs 5 points (180 mints per minute per App), and 100 concurrent requests. GitHub also states "No more than 2,000 OAuth access token requests per hour are allowed for GitHub Apps and OAuth apps", and it does not say whether an installation token mint counts as one of those requests. GitHub Enterprise Server disables rate limits by default. The Token Service counts mints per App against the lower of these bounds and raises an alert as the count approaches it.
- The Token Service needs a route to the host APIs it mints from. For a public host, it dials directly under a NetworkPolicy rule with the same exclusions as the gateway's upstream rule. For a host whose route is not `direct`, such as a private GitLab or a host behind a connector, and for every link provider endpoint under `user-credential-delivery-design.md`, whatever its route, it mints through `lenny-egress` on its own identity with the tenant asserted, so a private host's mint takes the same path as its fetch and the decision service sees each call to a tenant-registered endpoint. At level 1 such a call goes through the level 1 proxy for a public endpoint and the direct row for a private one, with the decision call on I3 carrying the same assertion. A tenant that holds an egress group mints through that group for every host, so a provider sees mints from the tenant's address. The decision service honours this assertion only for that tenant's VCS pool hosts and the mint endpoints its providers declare, such as `api.github.com` or an STS endpoint. An endpoint's or binding's CA applies to the mint too. Each mint call is bounded in time, response size, and redirects, as the resolver is, because the endpoint may be tenant-registered. The mint token cache is keyed on tenant and host as well as installation, repositories, and permissions, because a tenant could otherwise register a host that copies another tenant's identifiers and receive that tenant's cached token. At level 1, the Token Service dials a private endpoint directly through an `ipBlock` or selector row the chart renders, and it checks the decision's address against its own read of the registration, as the gateway and mirror do.

**The pre-mint step.** The Token Service's mint path runs a pre-mint step before its cache lookup. It takes the verified session and answers allow, refuse, or unavailable. It admits everything until `user-git-identity-design.md`'s UG-2 fills it, and it sits before the lookup because a check after it would reuse a cached token without running. The decision service also honours the tenant assertion on the mint path for a link provider's declared endpoints, including a `kind: oauth2` provider's token, revocation, and user info endpoints and its template hosts under `user-credential-delivery-design.md`.

**Provider capability levels.** Each provider declares what it can mint, which determines what Lenny allows by default.

| Level         | Providers                                                                                                                                                                                             | Token                                                         | Default use                                                                                                      |
| :------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `mint-scoped` | GitHub, GitHub Enterprise Server, AWS CodeCommit (STS session policy on the repository ARN)                                                                                                           | Short-lived and limited to named repositories and permissions | Fetch, push, and credential bindings                                                                               |
| `mint-coarse` | GitLab project access tokens (minted per session and revoked at session end, through a durable pending-revocation record retried with backoff, because expiry has day granularity and a private host can be unreachable at session end); Azure DevOps Entra ID tokens (about one hour, scoped to the identity's permissions) | Short-lived or revocable, but wider than one repository       | Fetch, push, and credential bindings, with `netagent` path enforcement narrowing use to the session's repositories |
| `stored`      | Bitbucket Cloud, Gitea, Forgejo, generic `git-https`, and GitLab without project tokens                                                                                                               | A long-lived token the tenant supplies                        | Fetch only, unless the tenant opts in to stored-token push                                                       |

**Stored-token push.** Some hosts cannot mint short-lived tokens. On those hosts, a tenant can push only with a long-lived token it stores in a credential pool, such as a personal access token, an app password, or a repository access token created by hand.

- `allowStoredTokenPush` on the VCS credential pool enables this. The tenant admin sets it, and only when the platform admin has granted the tenant the `storedTokenPush` class.
- When it is enabled, `netagent` fetches the stored token through I8 for each push and keeps it in memory. The agent UID never sees it.
- `netagent` limits the token's use to the session's writable repositories. That limit is enforced by Lenny code in the pod, whereas a `mint-scoped` limit is enforced by the host.

These risks remain:

- The token is usually wider than one repository. A personal access token often covers every repository its owner can write.
- A compromise of the `netagent` container, or of the node, exposes the token's full scope for its lifetime.
- Revocation is manual.

To reduce the risk, the documentation recommends the narrowest token each host offers: GitLab project tokens, Bitbucket repository access tokens, and Forgejo repository-specific tokens.

**Repository routing.** A VCS credential pool gains repository path prefixes (for example `github.com/acme/` and `github.com/acme-labs/`), so a tenant can hold one GitHub App installation per organization. This replaces the one-pool-per-host rule. Pools' prefixes may overlap only under a rule the write validator enforces rather than a unique index, and selection takes the longest prefix from the candidate set the caller supplies, so a later environment scoping needs no migration.

**Write scope.** A token is minted with write permission only when the session's plan declares the repository writable and the VCS credential pool allows writes. The mechanism has these steps:

1. **Declaration.** The workspace plan already marks each `gitClone` source with `auth.leaseScope` `vcs.<provider>.read` or `vcs.<provider>.write` (§14). The design adds `writableRepositories` for repositories the session pushes to without cloning them, such as a fork. At session bind, the gateway records the session's writable set: every source with a write scope plus `writableRepositories`, each checked against the tenant's repository allowlist and the pool's `allowWrite`. A plan that declares a repository outside those bounds is rejected at create.
2. **Detection.** git push is a smart HTTP request for the `git-receive-pack` service on a repository path. `netagent` sees the service name and the path inside the TLS it terminates for a covered host, or in an absolute-form plain HTTP request to one.
3. **Request.** `netagent` asks the Token Service on I8 for a write token for that repository. Fetches ask for read tokens, and a fetch never receives a write token on a `mint-scoped` provider. On `mint-coarse` and `stored` providers the token is wider, as step 5 states.
4. **Check.** The Token Service re-reads the pod's binding and asks `lenny-egress-authz` for the scope decision; the request is refused unless the repository is in the session's writable set. For a refused push, nothing is minted, and the push fails with 403.
5. **Mint.** The Token Service mints a token limited to that repository (`repository_ids: [<id>]`, `permissions: {contents: write}`) and caches it for its hour. The pool stores each allowlisted repository's numeric identifier, resolved when the admin adds it, because GitHub serves renamed repositories under their old names and a name is therefore not a stable identity. GitHub enforces the limit, so a token used outside `netagent` still cannot write elsewhere.
6. **Use.** `netagent` attaches the token to the push it forwards and keeps it in memory. The agent never receives it.

Tokens are minted per operation rather than at session start, so a session that never pushes never holds a write token, and a pod whose session ended can obtain none. GitHub requires `workflows: write` to push changes under `.github/workflows/`. The default token omits it, and a session gets it only when its plan requests it and the pool's `allowWorkflowEdits` permits it. `allowWorkflowEdits` is a capability per provider, and a pool write setting it is refused on a provider with no such permission, because `netagent` reads ref commands and never a pack. On those providers, such as GitLab with `.gitlab-ci.yml`, an agent can push a CI file to any branch it can write, and the tenant uses host-side CI protection. For `mint-coarse` and `stored` providers, step 5 yields a wider token, and step 4 plus `netagent` path enforcement are the only repository limit.

**Replaced pieces.** `cmd/git-credential-lenny`, the `lenny/vcs_token` tool, and §14's in-pod credential helper sentence are removed. Until EG-3 removes the tool, the intra-pod allow-list in `tenant-scoping-identity-design.md` TS-3 carries it.

**The gateway runs no git.** Today the gateway clones each `gitClone` source (`gitref.Clone`), packs the tree into an archive, decodes it through the archive gate in the gateway, and streams the files into the pod (§14 `gitClone` row, §7.1 step 12). The shipped gateway image has no git binary, so that path fails in every deployed image (F-14.1.37). The design removes it:

- The adapter runs the clone in the pod against the upstream URL, through the same `http.proxy` and bundle. In the sidecar model `/run/netagent` is also mounted read-only into the adapter container, and the adapter sets `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM` for its clone. It holds no credentials, because `netagent` attaches them. In the embedded model, the embedded adapter runs it at the agent UID. No test has run the adapter's clone on this path, so it is owed.
- Ref resolution (`resolvedCommitSha`) stays in the gateway and stays synchronous at plan bind, so the §14 contract and its errors (`GIT_CLONE_REF_UNRESOLVABLE` and `GIT_CLONE_REF_RESOLVE_TRANSIENT`) are unchanged and a bad ref costs no claimed pod. The gateway no longer runs `git ls-remote`; it sends a protocol v2 `ls-refs` request over smart HTTP in Go, with a protocol v0 `info/refs` fallback for a host that refuses v2 or answers it with a 200 and an empty body, as stock git's `http-backend` does. Round 6 found GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket Cloud, Gitea, Forgejo, kernel.org, and sourcehut all answering v2. The request is bounded: `ref-prefix` narrows the v2 advertisement, strict pkt-line parsing applies, the whole call is capped at 10 seconds and a v2 response at 1 MiB, and each tenant has a concurrency limit, so a hostile host cannot hold gateway goroutines or stream an unbounded advertisement. v0 cannot narrow, and the advertisements of `kubernetes/kubernetes` and `gitlab-org/gitlab-runner` exceed 1 MiB, so the v0 path streams under a 32 MiB cap, keeps only the candidate refs, and stops once the sorted advertisement passes them. Redirects are refused, with one exception: a redirect to the same scheme and host whose path differs only by a trailing `.git` is followed once, because GitLab answers the suffixless path with a 301. The request goes through `lenny-egress` on the gateway's own identity, carrying the tenant and the session being created, and the decision service applies that tenant's effective policy and routes to it, including a private git endpoint or an egress group, and requires the host to match one of the tenant's VCS pool prefixes or git routes. The read credential is attached only when the URL lies within a pool's repository prefix, which replaces today's `hostMatchesPatterns` wildcard match in `credentialpoolstore/vcsresolve.go`. One shared canonicalizer serves the gateway resolver, the create-time validation of `gitClone` and `writableRepositories`, and `netagent`'s path enforcement on a covered host: it lower-cases the host, drops userinfo and default ports, refuses non-default ports unless the prefix names one, resolves dot segments and percent-encoding, refuses `;` parameters and a trailing `.git` mismatch, and attaches a credential only to the canonical form, so `bitbucket.org/acme/../evil/repo` cannot carry a tenant's stored token outside the allowlist. When the tenant's git route for the host is the mirror, the gateway resolves against the mirror on the gateway's mTLS identity carrying the tenant and the session. The mirror forwards the same bounded `ls-refs` upstream and answers from that, so a session gets the host's current commit whether or not the store has caught up. The mirror's upstream call uses the gateway's 1.5-second CONNECT budget, so an Envoy outage reaches the store fallback quickly, and it has an 8-second limit overall, below the gateway's 10-second cap, and it coalesces identical `ls-refs` calls per store and ref for 5 seconds, so one tenant's volume does not multiply against a host that throttles by source address. It answers from the store only for an explicit list of causes: no reply from `lenny-egress`, a CONNECT 503, a failed connection or TLS handshake to the host, a timeout, or a host 502, 503, or 504 inside the tunnel. Every other answer is passed on and never falls back: a host 401, 403, 404, or 429, so a repository made private does not resolve from a public store, and a CONNECT 403 or 502, because `custom_response` turns a chained gateway's refusal into a 502. An unavailable mint counts as a listed cause when the provider or the Token Service is down, and a refused mint does not. The fallback also requires a confirmation within the last 6 hours (a Helm value). A confirmation is a (ref, SHA) pair recorded by a successful `ls-refs` or fetch, including a prefetch or a warm fill, and the store index persists it across a mirror restart. A successful answer whose `ref-prefix` covers a ref and omits it clears that ref's pair, so a deleted ref does not resolve during an outage, and other refs' pairs are untouched. The fallback answers only with the ref's most recent pair, and only when the store holds that commit, so a force-push seen by `ls-refs` just before a host failure returns the transient error rather than the retracted commit. A host 401, 403, or 404 and a refused mint stop the store's fallback and clear its confirmations until the next success, so a later 503 cannot serve a repository made private or removed from an App installation. The session records `resolvedFromStore` and the confirmation time, which `GET /v1/sessions/{id}` reports, so an old commit is visible and its age is bounded. Beyond that age the answer is `GIT_CLONE_REF_RESOLVE_TRANSIENT`. The mirror still asks I3 on every request, so the fallback covers upstream and `lenny-egress` outages only, and a decision service outage returns the transient error. When the answer names a commit the store lacks, the mirror schedules a fetch, so the first session for a new repository is not held by a full fetch; the pod's later fetch of an object the store lacks waits on that scheduled fetch: the mirror reads the `want` lines before it runs `upload-pack` and answers 503 with a retry hint when the covering fetch is still running. A `want` the store lacks with no covering fetch, which a plan that pins a full SHA produces because it skips resolution, schedules a fetch of that repository under the caller's binding and gets the same answer; a fetch that completes without the object ends the wait with a failure. An agent can trigger these misses by looping `git fetch origin <random-sha>`, so scheduling is bounded by a 60-second negative cache per store and SHA, which any completed fetch of the repository clears so a plan pinned to a newly pushed commit is not refused, a per-tenant rate on scheduled fetches (P7), and a minimum interval between fetches of one repository, which keeps one tenant from delaying a public store or spending the mirror's shared source address. git does not retry, so `netagent` holds the pod's request and retries the mirror with backoff for the pool's fetch retry window before it returns the failure to git. The window applies once per initial materialization, over every `gitClone` source of the plan together, and it must sit below the watchdog that bounds it: `maxFinalizingTimeoutSeconds` (600 s by default, §6.2) less the Runtime's setup bound (`setupPolicy.timeoutSeconds`, 300 s by default), less 60 s, which admits the 180 s default. The pool validator reads the Runtime for that bound, taking the derived-runtime maximum where one applies (§5.1) and the §6.2 default for a pool without a Runtime reference, and a Runtime update that raises the setup bound is refused while any pool referencing it holds a window the new bound no longer admits, with the refusal naming the pools. On a pool copied from a template under `tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`, a template write that raises the window succeeds, and the owned pool's effective window is the smaller of the copied value and what its setup bound admits. A snapshotless resume rebuild runs the clone and the setup commands under the 300 s `resuming` watchdog (§7.3), which leaves no room for the window, so on the resume path `netagent` waits 30 s at most and then fails fast; a resumed session's repositories were fetched when the session first ran, so a pending fetch there means the mirror lost its store, and the next resume attempt succeeds once the fetch completes. The mirror's fetch continues after the pod gives up, so a repository too large for the window fails the first session at finalize as `SESSION_CREATION_FAILED` with the reason `git_fetch_pending` (§6.2 surfaces materialization failures there, and the `GIT_CLONE_REF_*` codes keep their plan-bind meaning) and serves the next one from the store; the gateway's `ls-refs` miss at plan bind already starts that fetch before the pod is claimed, which covers most repositories. For a large repository a tenant admin prefetches it through the mirror prefetch resource of the admin API (a tenant-scoped request naming a repository and the pool it falls under, which the mirror fetches into the tenant's store, or into the public store for an anonymous fetch), or lists it as a `gitRepository` warm fill, so no session pays for its first fetch. An upstream outage therefore blocks session creation only for refs the upstream has not confirmed within the age limit. The SHA then travels to the pod in the plan, and the adapter clones that SHA. No SHA reported from the pod is trusted.
- Resolution depends on the egress path at session create, and round 6 ran that path through Envoy against real hosts. The gateway mounts a projected token with the `lenny-egress` audience and has no fallback that dials a host directly.
  - **One tunnel per call.** The client disables connection reuse. With reuse, 20 calls alternating between two tenants produced one decision, attributed to the first tenant, so the second tenant's policy and routes were never consulted.
  - **Attempts.** Each attempt must receive its CONNECT reply within 1.5 seconds, and a failed attempt retries once on a new connection, inside the 10-second cap. A stopped or frozen Envoy replica under load then caused no failed call, at a cost of 1.5 seconds at worst.
  - **Errors.** No CONNECT reply, a proxy 503, 502, or 429, the time cap, and a host 5xx or 429 map to `GIT_CLONE_REF_RESOLVE_TRANSIENT`, and a proxy 502 also raises an alert because it can mean a chained gateway refused Lenny's credential. A CONNECT 403 or 407 maps to `GIT_CLONE_REF_UNRESOLVABLE` with the reason `egress_denied`, and a host 401, 403, or 404, the byte cap, a malformed response, a refused redirect, and an absent ref map to `GIT_CLONE_REF_UNRESOLVABLE`. A host 401 or 403 carries the reason `auth_failed`, and a 404 carries `not_found`, because hosts answer 404 for a private repository the caller cannot see. The CONNECT reply decides the class, because a dial timeout in Go also satisfies `context.DeadlineExceeded` and would otherwise read as a slow host.
  - **Private git endpoints.** The resolver reaches a platform-registered private endpoint the way pods do, as a raw tunnel through `lenny-egress` to the address the decision pins, so it shares their availability. Level 1 differs, as "Level 1 replacement" states.
  - **Scope of the dependency.** Sessions without `gitClone`, and plans that pin a full commit SHA, need no resolution and are unaffected. During a `lenny-egress` outage, callers get a retryable error within 3 seconds, and a mirror-routed repository resolves from the store within its age limit. During a decision service outage every resolution returns the retryable error. Pods that clone through `lenny-egress` fail closed in the same outage (P6), so for them the dependency adds no outage that a directly dialing resolver would have avoided. A pod that clones from the mirror can still clone during a `lenny-egress` outage, and its session can start when the store resolves the ref.
  - **Cost.** Envoy added about 0.3 ms per call against a local host, and the hosts' own latency (280 to 390 ms at p50) dominated against real ones. The path served 4,271 calls per second with a p99 of 130 ms on a laptop, far above any session create rate.
  - **Deployments without a central proxy.** A plan that needs resolution fails with `GIT_CLONE_REF_UNRESOLVABLE` and the reason `no_egress_path`. Level 2 proxies must pass `x-lenny-tenant`, the terminated marker, and the asserted route to their I3 call, and a conformance row tests each. Level 1 follows "Level 1 replacement".
- F-14.1.37 is closed by deleting the gateway clone path rather than by adding git to the gateway image. The adapter image, and the embedded runtime images that run the clone, need a git binary. The adapter image is distroless today.
- A session whose plan has a `gitClone` source is rejected at create when its pool has no git path (a `restricted` pool without `gitAccess`).
- A default clone on a tenant-derived runtime (`tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`, DR-9) is a `gitClone` source once composed, and these rules apply to it. That design also runs the resolver's host check on a default clone with a full commit. A default clone's `write` value adds to the session's writable set at bind, under the rules here, and a warm clone fills a store on the owned pool, under "Warm clones" in that design.
- The adapter clones into a new empty directory, with hooks and repository configuration ignored, and runs all its git work there, including setting `origin`. It then moves the working tree to the target under §14's path collision rule and replaces any `.git` at the target as a unit, and it runs no git in the target afterwards, apart from draft 0111's capture copy and restore completion, which use a git directory and configuration the adapter creates for each operation. A client's clone at `.` meets default files and default clones, and a `.git/config`, `commondir`, or hook already in the target must not run anything at the adapter's UID, which holds the service account token, and must not survive the move. Every materializer write, move, and removal opens its path with `openat2` under `RESOLVE_BENEATH | RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS`, because a restored symbolic link chain passes the lexical target checks (F-13.4.24). Before every checkout, the adapter streams the tree's listing under a git directory of its own, with each entry charged as 0111's rule 2 charges it and the total running across the plan, and it refuses at `workspaceSizeLimitBytes`, `adapter.maxCloneTreeEntries`, or a time limit, because a fetch cap bounds packed bytes alone, a tree can reuse subtrees, and a commit a store holds causes no fetch. The slot volume's `sizeLimit` is the second line. Proposal 3 tests this, including alternates under the move, which 0111 writes as an absolute path. No test has run it.
- The tree checks that the archive gate applied are covered as described under "Residual risks and limits", **Tree validation**.
