The 0111 open item on workspace ownership carries over: a repository the adapter checks out or restores from a snapshot is owned by UID 65532, so the runtime UID's git refuses it as dubious ownership unless the checkout is made group-writable and owned for the runtime. Proposal 3 resolves it.

### Push limits and commit identity

- **`allowedRefPatterns`** on a VCS pool, and on a session's `writableRepositories` entry within it, limits which refs a session can push. Hosts cannot enforce this through a repository-scoped token, so `netagent` reads the ref update commands of `git-receive-pack`, which it already intercepts, and refuses the whole push if any ref falls outside the patterns. It also refuses ref deletion unless the pool allows it. Whether a push is forced cannot be determined from the commands alone, so that remains for the host's branch protection. The limit is enforced in the pod, and a tenant's host-side branch protection remains the outside control. Each push writes a `git.operation` record.
- **Push options.** `netagent` refuses a `git-receive-pack` request using the `push-options` capability and strips that capability from the ref advertisement, so git reports the failure before sending a pack. A per-pool `allowedPushOptions` list, empty by default, can admit an option. GitLab's page lists `merge_request.auto_merge`, `ci.skip`, `secret_push_protection.skip_all`, and `security_policy.bypass_reason`. This needs bounded parsing of the section between the ref commands and the pack, which `netagent` reads on the terminated connection.
- **What the limit covers.** It covers git on a covered host, by any route to `netagent`: under the rendered config, with `http.proxy` overridden to the same listener, by `curl` over HTTPS through the proxy, and in an absolute-form plain HTTP request. With `http.proxy` unset there is no route, except on an `internet` pool, whose raw sockets bypass `netagent`, and at the egress proxy with no credential the answer is 407 **[measured]**. On an uncovered host the limit does not hold, as **Uncovered git hosts** states. A write-scoped REST credential can also create refs through a host's API, which is a stated residual, and host-side branch protection is the outside control.
- **Commit identity.** A VCS pool can set the author and committer that the git configuration gives a session, so agent commits are distinguishable and consistent.
- **GitLab bot accounts.** A GitLab project access token creates a bot user, so minting one per session fills the instance with bots. A pool can therefore reuse tokens, keyed on tenant, pool, project, and scope, so a read session never holds a write-capable token and no token crosses a tenant. A reused token rotates at a set lifetime, 7 days by default, and is revoked when the repository leaves the allowlist, `allowWrite` turns off, the pool is deleted, the tenant is erased, or an admin asks. A reused token outlives the session, so a compromised `netagent` could keep it until then, and it is an exception to P2. Reuse therefore needs the `storedTokenPush` grant, and per-session minting stays the default.


## Credential delivery to runtimes

Pull requests, issues, releases, private package registries, and any other HTTP API a runtime calls go through the egress route with standard HTTP. Lenny adds no platform tool for them, and the delivery mechanism is generic: it knows hosts, headers, files, and request rules held as data, and it has no code for GitHub or any other product on this path. The one exception is git classification on every terminated host, so a marker match on a bound non-git API path, such as a path holding `.git/`, is refused.

### Credential bindings

A tenant admin configures access with a **credential binding**:

- `secret`: the source of the value, a typed union with a `kind`. It is a credential pool whose provider mints a token (for example a GitHub App, with a `permissions` ceiling such as `pull_requests: write`), a stored secret in the credential namespace, such as an API key, or `userCredential`, which names a tenant-defined type so that each session receives its own user's token (see `user-credential-delivery-design.md`). The Token Service, `netagent`, and the decision service treat an unknown kind as absent, as they do `suspendedBy`.
- `hosts`: the exact hosts the value may be sent to. On a `userCredential` binding, these are a subset of the type's hosts, and host rules evaluate the type's hosts. Wildcards are refused, and each host must lie within the tenant's effective allow set, so a prompt-injected agent cannot make the tenant's key travel to a host the tenant never allowed. `netagent` never attaches the value to a request for any other host. A binding whose `secret` is a stored token, under either delivery, which includes a `userCredential` type with `source: registered`, is refused when any of its hosts is a git host that one of the tenant's VCS pools covers, because the long-lived token would otherwise reach that host's REST write routes with no path enforcement and outside the `storedTokenPush` grant; git on those hosts takes the git path with the pool's credential, and API access to them uses a minted token or a binding on the host's API hostname. The one exception is a `userCredential` binding under `file`, which `user-credential-delivery-design.md` states. On the API host of a pool with `userAuthorization: required`, a stored secret is refused too, because it has no check, and setting `required` on a pool suspends one that exists. A `userCredential` binding carries no CA and no `attach` of its own. Its `attach` comes from its type or link provider, and its CA from the host's endpoint or outbound host record, whose digest the token trust record holds.
- `attach`: how the value goes on the request, as a header template. Examples are `Authorization: Bearer {{secret}}`, `Authorization: Basic {{base64 user ":" secret}}`, and `X-Api-Key: {{secret}}`.
- `placeholder`: the non-secret value a tool sees in place of the secret. It defaults to the constant `lenny-injected` (see **Placeholders under `inject`.** below).
- `scope`: `always`, which attaches the binding to every session of the tenant, `onRequest`, which attaches it only to a session whose plan names it, or `poolAttach`, which a VCS pool's `prAuthoring: user` sets and which `user-git-identity-design.md`, "The attach rule", defines. A `userCredential` binding cannot be `always`.
- `delivery`: `inject`, `federation`, `metadata`, or `file`. A `userCredential` binding takes `inject` or `file`.
- `requestRules`: optional, under `inject` only, data that refuses requests before the value is attached (see **Request inspection stage.** below).
- `audience` and a provider template, for `federation` delivery.
- `projections`: for `file` delivery, the extra file formats to render (see **Which files `netagent` writes.** below).

A session names its `onRequest` bindings and, for a minted token, can narrow the permissions. A mint for a binding with any write permission names only repositories with a `granted` writable entry. The session does not enumerate APIs. One binding covers one secret and its hosts, however many endpoints those hosts expose. A session holds at most one `inject` binding per host and at most one `userCredential` binding per type host, under any delivery, and a `file` binding beside an `inject` binding on one host is refused too, each at bind and evaluated on type hosts for a `userCredential` binding, because `netagent` would otherwise overwrite one with the other and an App binding could answer in place of a user's.

### Delivery modes

| Mode | Where the secret is | Who can read it | Rotation | Availability |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `inject` (default) | `netagent` memory only. The pod sees the placeholder. | No process in the runtime or adapter container | Transparent to the tool | Always available |
| `federation` | A 15-minute Lenny-issued JWT in a file, which the provider's SDK exchanges at the provider | Any process at the agent UID | `netagent` rewrites the token, and the SDKs re-read it on each exchange | Tenant opt-in behind the `agentReadableDelivery` grant. It is an exception to P3, limited to a short-lived token bounded by the tenant's trust conditions. |
| `metadata` | `netagent` memory, served on loopback in a provider's metadata endpoint format | Any process at the agent UID that reads the endpoint's token file | Each fetch returns current credentials, and the SDKs refresh before expiry | Tenant opt-in behind the `agentReadableDelivery` grant. A provider plugin; AWS ships first. |
| `file` | A file in `/run/netagent/credentials`, written by `netagent` | Any process at the agent UID | `netagent` swaps the directory atomically, and the tested tools re-read their files on each invocation | Tenant opt-in behind the `agentReadableDelivery` grant, or `userCredentialDelivery` for a `userCredential` binding. It is an exception to P3. |
| Environment variable | Never used | n/a | n/a | Never offered |

**`inject`** keeps the secret out of the pod. The tool sends a request carrying the placeholder, or no credential, and `netagent` sets the header from the `attach` template when the request is for one of the binding's hosts. The check is on the request's authority, derived once: `:authority` on HTTP/2, and on HTTP/1.1 the request-target's host when the request is in absolute form, with a `Host` header that disagrees refused. The authority, with its port, must equal the bound host exactly, and the check is not on the CONNECT authority alone: on a shared edge, a request for `attacker.example` inside a connection to `api.example.com` would otherwise carry the injected header to the attacker's origin. `netagent` opens the upstream connection with the bound host as the server name and validates the upstream certificate against the public roots and the deployer CA only, never against the per-pod CA or a `tenantOverride.caBundle`, which sits in the per-pool bundle and in no record. A binding or endpoint that carries its own CA adds that CA for its hosts only. `netagent` overwrites the header rather than substituting text inside it, so encoded forms such as Basic authentication work, and a placeholder copied into a request body or query is never replaced. A request to any other host leaves `netagent` unchanged, where the placeholder is meaningless. A stage between the authority check and the attach step inspects the request (see **Request inspection stage.** below). Two transports reach `netagent` in plain text:

- **Plain HTTP to a bound host.** A tool configured to call `http://<host>/...` sends an absolute-form request through `HTTP_PROXY`. `netagent` attaches the header and forwards the request over TLS through `lenny-egress`, so the cleartext stays on loopback. No CA is involved. Fly.io's tokenizer uses the same upgrade. `netagent` reads these requests' heads directly, because Go's `net/http` drops `Host` on an absolute-form request, and the check that `Host` and the target agree needs both. A smart HTTP or LFS path to a covered host takes the git path, and the rules for pinned and read-only hosts apply, as they do inside a terminated tunnel. The third validation round showed that `glab` with `api_protocol: http` sends exactly this request through `HTTP_PROXY`, and that curl does so for `http://` URLs.
- **TLS termination for bound and covered hosts only.** For tools that hard-code HTTPS (for example `gh`, and git), `netagent` terminates TLS for the binding's hosts and the tenant's covered hosts with a per-pod CA and passes every other host through as an opaque tunnel. Each client CONNECT to a terminated host gets its own decision, `netagent` reuses a pooled upstream connection only for a CONNECT that decision allows, and a deny closes the pool for that host, because round 12 saw three new CONNECTs succeed with no decision after the policy stopped allowing the host. `netagent` generates the CA at first start and keeps its key in its private emptyDir, so a restart reuses it and the runtime's trust stays valid. The CA carries X.509 name constraints that permit only the hosts `netagent` terminates: those of the pod's `inject` bindings, its covered hosts, and any pinned or read-only hosts, and `netagent` re-issues it when a binding adds a host. A process that loaded the anchor before a host was known does not see the new constraints, and a client that enforces them refuses that host. The anchor therefore carries name constraints only when the runtime launches after bind and serves one binding set. In every other case (SDK-warm, service mode, embedded, a `keep` recycled pod, and concurrent slots) it carries none, and a leaked key could sign any name in that pod, which is the limit the JVM case already states. `netagent` publishes the generation it has applied in the network descriptor, and the adapter waits for the session's generation before materialization, setup, or launching the runtime, up to the fetch retry window, so a constrained anchor is current when a process loads it. If the wait ends first, the session fails at finalize with `SESSION_CREATION_FAILED` and the reason `network_not_ready`. OpenSSL, Go, rustls, BoringSSL, GnuTLS, NSS, .NET, and Chromium enforce constraints on a trust anchor, so for those clients a leaked key cannot sign for another DNS name. The CA also excludes every IP range, because a DNS-only constraint admits a leaf for an IP address in all of those except GnuTLS. A subdomain of a permitted host stays within the constraint in all of them, because X.509 has no exact-host form. Java's PKIX ignores a trust anchor's extensions, so JVM clients would trust a leaked key for any name; the limit is per pod and matters only after a `netagent` or node compromise, and the documentation states it. It writes the certificate into `/run/netagent` before the runtime starts (native sidecar ordering, validated in round 3), and the client proxy profile adds it to the CA trust variables. Vercel Sandbox, Cloudflare Sandbox, Daytona, E2B, and the Claude Code cloud GitHub proxy use the same pattern. Round 3 validated it with mitmproxy 12.3.1 standing in for `netagent` (see "Validation record"), and the spike ran git, `gh`, and `glab` through it on a stub.

**Request inspection stage.** Between the authority check and the attach step, on both transports, `netagent` runs a stage receiving the method, raw path, headers, and a bounded body reader, which can refuse the request or replace the forwarded path, headers, body, and query string. With no rules it admits everything unchanged and streams. A binding's `requestRules` reach `netagent` resolved on I8, as data, with no provider code in `netagent`: REST method and path patterns, GraphQL top-level mutation names, body text patterns, per-flavour paths, request forms, and a default of allow or deny. A binding whose rules have no `preset` key, on the API host of a VCS pool, resolves to the preset of the flavour that pool's provider and host derive, and only `preset: none` clears it. Classification runs first, under "Git on covered hosts", so a smart HTTP path on a terminated host never reaches this stage. Under rules, `netagent` fails closed on path spelling and forwards the canonical path. They apply under `inject` alone, and they cannot apply to a token the runtime holds. Unparseable GraphQL is refused, and the printed document is forwarded. Under rules, `netagent` strips credential headers and forwards the rest. Presets of rules ship as data, as the provider templates do. `user-credential-delivery-design.md` defines all of this, including who can clear a preset, and `user-git-identity-design.md` holds the evidence. A rule refusal goes to `egress.local`.

`inject` cannot serve these cases, which need `federation`, `metadata`, or `file` delivery:

- Request signing computed from the secret on the client, such as cloud request signing (`federation` or `metadata`) or HMAC-signed requests (`file`).
- mTLS client certificates.
- Tools that pin certificates or use compiled-in roots, under TLS termination.
- Tools that validate the token format locally and reject the placeholder.
- OAuth flows in which the client must hold a refresh token.

A response that reflects the secret (an echo endpoint, or an error that quotes the request) would carry it into the pod. `netagent` scrubs the value and each rendered form of it from response headers, bodies, and WebSocket server frames on bound hosts, because a reflected Basic header carries the secret only in encoded form, which round 10 showed the exact value misses:

- It rewrites `Accept-Encoding` to the encodings it can decode and refuses a response in any other, because a reflection compressed with `zstd` otherwise passes through unscrubbed. It decodes `gzip` and `deflate` bodies before scrubbing. Round 3 confirmed that reflections in plain, compressed, and finely chunked bodies never reached the client.
- A streaming response (SSE, or any body `netagent` does not buffer) is scrubbed with a window that holds only a suffix that is a proper prefix of the secret, so a secret split across chunks is still caught and an event that cannot begin a secret passes at once. Round 10 tested this against the fixed window first proposed here, which held back one byte less than the secret's length and so withheld each event's end until the next bytes: 503 ms of lag at 500 ms spacing and 10.2 s across a pause, against 1.1 ms and 2.7 ms for the prefix window, with no leak in the reflection runs, including 5-byte and 7-byte chunks and flushed gzip. Buffering the whole body instead delayed every SSE event to the end of the stream.
- WebSocket frames from the server need their own scrub. Without it, a reflected secret in a frame reached the client.
- A reflection re-encoded by the server, such as base64 or hex, defeats the scrub. The documentation states this as a residual risk.

`netagent` also strips hop-by-hop headers such as `Proxy-Connection` before forwarding, which mitmproxy passed upstream in the plain-HTTP test.

**Gates.** The opt-ins that place a credential in the agent's reach fall into risk classes, and each class has its own gate: `storedTokenPush` (a long-lived secret held by `netagent`), `agentReadableDelivery` (`file`, `federation`, and `metadata` of a tenant's credential), `userCredentialDelivery` (`file` of a person's token, under a `userCredential` binding, as `user-credential-delivery-design.md` defines), and `verbatimClientFiles`. `tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md` adds `tenantDerivedRuntimes`, a gate of this form with parameters. A gate is a platform-admin grant per tenant, not a deployment-wide switch, and the tenant admin then opts in per binding or pool, so enabling `file` for one tenant enables nothing for another and nothing in another class.

**`federation`** gives a runtime a short-lived identity token that it exchanges itself, at the provider, for the provider's own credentials. It serves SDKs that sign requests and so cannot use `inject`, and it is provider-neutral: Lenny's part is one JWT issuer and a set of file templates, and every provider that accepts OIDC workload identity federation consumes it through its own SDK. The clouds (AWS, GCP, and Azure), HashiCorp Vault, and most secrets managers accept it, and Kubernetes itself uses the same mechanism for its own workload identity.

- **Issuer.** The Token Service is an OIDC issuer with one issuer URL per tenant, `https://<lenny>/oidc/<tenant-id>`, keyed by the tenant identifier rather than the name. The identifier is caller-supplied at creation (`admin/tenants.go`), and the §12.8 tenant tombstone prevents its reuse, so a deleted and recreated tenant cannot inherit the trust the old one's admin configured; the issuer's uniqueness rests on that tombstone. Each tenant has its own federation key set, held in the Token Service's key store (`kms.go`) beside its other keys, signing nothing else, and destroyed when the tenant is deleted, so a federation token is useless at any Lenny endpoint and dies with its tenant. Rotation keeps the previous key published for the token lifetime plus the providers' JWKS caching period. The `/oidc/` path on the gateway ingress is reserved for the Token Service, and discovery must be reachable by the providers' token endpoints, which means a publicly routable ingress with a publicly trusted certificate; a deployment without one cannot offer `federation`, and uses `metadata` instead. A tenant's trust configuration names its own issuer, so another tenant's tokens fail the issuer check by construction, and the tenant admin needs no claim condition to keep P4. A binding deleted and recreated under the same name keeps its subject, so the tenant admin treats a binding name as the identity its provider role trusts.
- **Token.** For a session with a `federation` binding, `netagent` requests a JWT from the Token Service on I8 and writes it to `/run/netagent/credentials/<name>/token`. The claims are the tenant's issuer, the binding's `audience`, the stable subject `lenny:<tenant>:<binding>`, and separate `pool` and `session` claims for audit. The subject is stable because AWS trust policies condition only on `aud`, `sub`, and `amr`, and Azure requires an exact subject match. The token lasts 15 minutes, and `netagent` rewrites it at two thirds of its lifetime. The SDKs re-read the file on each exchange; the Azure SDKs cache it for 5 to 10 minutes, which is within that lifetime.
- **Trust.** The tenant admin configures the provider to trust the tenant's issuer for that audience and subject, and grants the resulting identity the provider role or permissions the agent needs. This is the same step a platform team performs for a Kubernetes cluster's workload identity today. Where the provider supports it (AWS `aws:SourceIp`, GCP and Azure conditional access), the tenant also binds the exchange to the egress address of its egress group, which makes a token taken out of the pod useless elsewhere.
- **Client configuration.** The binding's provider template renders the provider's standard client configuration file into the binding's directory, naming the token path. The profile sets the constant variable that names that file. Lenny ships templates for the three clouds as data, and a deployer adds others.

| Provider | File rendered | Launch variable | Per-tenant values | Validation (round 4) |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| AWS | A shared config profile with `role_arn`, `web_identity_token_file`, `role_session_name`, `region`, and optional `services` endpoints | `AWS_CONFIG_FILE` | All in the file | boto3, AWS SDK for Go v2, JavaScript v3, and Java v2 assumed the role, signed requests, and re-read a changed token on refresh |
| GCP | An `external_account` JSON with `audience`, `token_url`, `credential_source.file`, and optional impersonation | `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS` | All in the file, except the project lookup below | google-auth for Python, Node, and Go exchanged the token and re-read it on refresh |
| Azure | None. The SDKs read `AZURE_TENANT_ID`, `AZURE_CLIENT_ID`, and `AZURE_FEDERATED_TOKEN_FILE` from the environment only | The three variables | The tenant ID and client ID cannot come from a file | azure-identity for Python, Node, and Go exchanged the token and re-read it after their cache expired |

Two caveats come from the validation:

- The Python and Node Google libraries look up the project at `cloudresourcemanager.googleapis.com` during initialization. The `googleapis` host set includes that host, so the lookup succeeds through the proxy when the policy allows it. Otherwise the binding sets `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT`, which then becomes a per-tenant launch variable with the limit below.
- Azure's identifiers are per-tenant launch variables, and launch variables cannot vary per tenant on a shared pool. An Azure `federation` binding therefore works on a tenant-owned pool, or in runtime code that passes the identifiers from `binding.json` to the credential constructor through the SDK accessor. The validation confirmed that all three Azure SDKs accept them as constructor arguments.

The JWT is readable by any process at the agent UID, which makes `federation` an exception to P3 in the same way as `file`, limited to a 15-minute token whose use the tenant's trust conditions bound. The `agentReadableDelivery` grant controls it. The provider exchange is an HTTPS request to the provider's token endpoint, which the effective policy must allow. A token taken out of the pod can be exchanged from anywhere within its lifetime unless the trust binds the source address, so the documentation recommends that binding and roles with the narrowest permissions.

**`metadata`** emulates a provider's instance metadata or container credentials endpoint on loopback, for a tenant that cannot configure federation trust. The Token Service holds the provider credential and mints the short-lived credentials that `netagent` serves. Round 3 and round 4 validated the three formats: the AWS container credentials endpoint with boto3, the AWS CLI, and the Go, JavaScript, and Java SDKs; the GCE metadata server with google-auth for Python, Node, and Go; and the Azure App Service managed identity endpoint with azure-identity for Python, Node, and Go. Each format needs its own paths, headers, and response document, so `metadata` is a provider plugin in `netagent` with a stated interface rather than data. Lenny ships the AWS plugin, because AWS is the one provider whose endpoint the SDKs restrict to loopback, and the other two follow when a deployer needs them. Three details from the validation hold for every plugin: a token file must not end in a newline, the Python Google library needs `GCE_METADATA_IP` as well as `GCE_METADATA_HOST`, and the emulator must serve each probe path the SDK sends before the token path.

**`file`** writes the secret into the pod.

No standard governs how a platform hands a secret to a workload as a file. The practices in common use are these:

| Practice | Examples | Fit for Lenny |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| One file per secret in a directory that an environment variable names | Kubernetes Secret volumes, Docker and Compose secrets in `/run/secrets/<name>`, and systemd credentials in `$CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY` | Generic. Any program can read a file, and Kubernetes rotates such directories by an atomic symlink swap. |
| `.netrc` | curl, git, GNU wget, Python requests, pip, and the Go toolchain | Host-scoped Basic credentials only. Round 3 found uneven support: curl needs `--netrc`, httpx needs explicit `NetRCAuth`, and BusyBox wget, npm, Node fetch, and Java ignore it. Only requests, pip, and Go read the `NETRC` variable. |
| OIDC workload identity federation | Kubernetes service account tokens consumed by AWS IAM Roles for Service Accounts, GKE Workload Identity, Azure Workload Identity, and Vault JWT auth | A standard, provider-neutral mechanism with one issuer on the platform side. This is the `federation` mode. |
| Cloud workload credential endpoints | AWS container credentials, the GCP metadata server, and the Azure managed identity endpoint | Standard within each cloud's SDKs, and short-lived, but each has its own format. This is the `metadata` mode. |
| Tool-native config files | `gh` `hosts.yml`, `.npmrc`, `pip.conf`, and Docker `config.json` | Each tool owns its format. |
| Exec credential helpers | git credential helpers, Docker credential helpers, kubectl exec plugins, and AWS `credential_process` | Each is a tool-specific protocol, and each needs a helper binary in the image. |

The `file` contract follows the first practice:

- The launch environment sets `LENNY_CREDENTIALS_DIR=/run/netagent/credentials`. The path sits outside `/run/lenny`, which §13.1 reserves for the credential tmpfs.
- Each `file` binding of the session gets a directory, `<name>/`. It holds `value`, the raw secret with no trailing newline, and `binding.json`, with the name, hosts, `attach` template, `expiresAt`, and a version counter.
- `netagent` replaces a binding's directory atomically by writing a new directory and swapping a symlink, as the kubelet does for Secret volumes. A reader that opens the file on each use always sees a complete value.
- The files have mode 0444 in an emptyDir that only `netagent` writes, and the runtime container mounts it read-only.

**Which files `netagent` writes.** `netagent` writes the generic directory for each `file` binding and nothing else, unless the binding lists `projections`.

- Lenny ships one projection, `netrc`. It is a single file at `/run/netagent/credentials/netrc` with an entry for each binding whose `attach` template is Basic, and the profile sets `NETRC` to it. It serves requests, pip, and Go.
- Lenny ships no tool-specific projections. A deployer adds one as a file template in the client proxy profile, such as a `gh` `hosts.yml`.
- `glab` refuses a config file unless its mode is exactly 0600, which a file written by the `netagent` UID cannot give the agent UID. `glab` therefore uses `inject` with TLS termination, configured through `GITLAB_HOST` and a `GITLAB_TOKEN` placeholder.

**Translation by the runtime.** A runtime that builds its own clients reads the generic directory, through the SDK accessor (see "Runtime SDKs") or directly, and configures those clients itself. Tool knowledge then stays with the runtime author, who knows which tools the image carries. Lenny adds no template engine or tool formats to the SDKs, for two reasons:

- A command the agent runs in a shell never passes through runtime code, so a translation in the runtime would not reach it.
- A rendering step in the runtime would have to watch for rotation.

**Placeholders under `inject`.** `inject` needs no file. The tested tools send no credential, or a placeholder, and `netagent` overwrites the header. A tool that refuses to run without a token (round 3: `gh`) gets a placeholder through an environment variable in the profile, such as `GH_TOKEN` or `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN`. `netagent` ignores the placeholder's value, so the placeholder is the constant `lenny-injected` rather than a per-binding value, and it can be fixed at pod creation.

**Environment variables** are never used for secrets, and §5.1 already forbids them. Compared with a file, an environment variable has these additional exposures:

- `/proc/<pid>/environ` keeps the start-time value even after the process unsets it.
- Every child process inherits it.
- Diagnostic reports include it, such as Node's report on an uncaught exception.
- The Lenny supervisor renders the runtime's environment as command-line arguments.
- It cannot be rotated without restarting the process.

OWASP's secrets management guidance and CWE-526 make the same recommendation. A placeholder in an environment variable is not a secret, so `inject` can expose one as `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN=lenny-injected`. Round 3 confirmed that `gh` refuses to run with no token and runs with an environment token.

### Exfiltration controls

These controls land in EG-12, after the other proposals, and bytes out lands with the `egress.tunnel` stream. Hostname filtering leaves opaque tunnels, so an allowed host can carry data out (see "Residual risks and limits"). A published attack sent files to a trusted LLM API using an attacker's key. These controls narrow that channel for hosts where the platform or tenant turns them on:

- **Credential-pinned hosts.** A host set or binding can set `credentialPinned`, and a `required` VCS pool under `user-git-identity-design.md` is a source of pins for its host and API hosts, with the per-session exception that document states. `netagent` then terminates TLS for those hosts, as it does for a bound host, and refuses a request when the host has no binding. An attacker's key cannot be used there, under these rules:
  - The match uses the shared canonicalizer (case and default port), so `API.EXAMPLE.COM:443` cannot miss the pin and get an opaque tunnel. Inside a pinned connection, a request whose authority differs is refused, where `inject` alone would pass it through without attaching a secret.
  - The binding's template overwrites its header, and a per-host credential list strips other credential headers and query parameters, such as `?private_token=`. The list is data in the host set, and a host with no list strips every header that the shipped list names.
  - WebSocket and other upgrades are refused, because their frames escape these checks.
  - On a pinned git host with no binding, a smart HTTP path takes the git path first, and other requests are refused. A read-only host admits `info/refs` and `git-upload-pack` as reads and refuses `git-receive-pack`, because classification runs before the method rule.
  - A pin names host names, and a provider's alias hosts are pinned with it from host data. Another name for the same service, or an agent's own name pointing at the host's address, is outside it, as **Uncovered git hosts** states.
  - The pinned hosts come from the Token Service on I8, as a binding's `hosts` do, so a compromised decision service cannot remove a pin. The decision service reads the same definitions and refuses a pod's CONNECT to a pinned, read-only, or covered host unless `netagent` marks it as coming from a terminated connection, with a header that `netagent` sets and strips from agent input. Service identities (the gateway resolver, the mirror, and the Token Service) have no `netagent` and carry no agent-supplied credential, so the check does not apply to them. A level 2 proxy must pass this header to its I3 call and drop it toward any upstream, as `lenny-egress` does. A level 2 proxy must also pass the asserted route, under "Credentials", **Deployments without a central proxy**. A `netagent` that has not read its list yet, for any reason, therefore gets a 403 for those hosts instead of an opaque tunnel, and no other host is affected. The marker for covered hosts lands in EG-3, and this control adds pinned and read-only hosts to it. A compromised `netagent` can forge the marker, and a compromised decision service can allow the tunnel, which are the stated limits of each. A trailing dot canonicalizes away.
  - Two limits remain and are stated in the documentation: a credential carried in a request body, and an upload the binding's own key authorizes into a place an attacker can read.
- **Read-only hosts.** Under the same termination and the same list source, a host can allow only `GET` and `HEAD`, which closes publish and upload paths on a registry the pool only needs to read. A tool that needs `POST` for reads must be listed separately.
- **Bytes out.** Each `egress.tunnel` record carries bytes in each direction, and an alert fires on a per-session threshold. This detects and does not prevent, and it cannot see inside a tunnel. `netagent` local tunnel accounting serves the same purpose at level 1.

These controls act in `netagent`, so they apply to traffic that passes through it. An `internet` pool permits raw sockets that bypass it, so the validators refuse a host set's or binding's pin on `internet` pools, and that pool's sessions stay outside the pin from a `required` VCS pool, which posture reports.


## Interfaces

| Interface                 | Between                                                                    | Protocol                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Defined by                                |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- |
| I1. Local egress endpoint | tools in the pod, including git, and `netagent` | HTTP CONNECT and absolute-form plain HTTP on loopback, no authentication | Standard |
| I2. Proxy hop             | `netagent` and the central proxy (Lenny default or a deployer replacement) | HTTP CONNECT over TLS with `Proxy-Authorization: Basic`. The password is the service account token for the default or a level 2 proxy, and the identity's current credential for a level 1 proxy.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Standard                                  |
| I3. Decision API          | proxy, git endpoint, or Token Service and `lenny-egress-authz`             | Envoy ext_authz v3, in both its gRPC and HTTP forms. A documented mapping to the OpenID AuthZEN Authorization API 1.0 (final since 2026-01-12) is optional, because no proxy calls AuthZEN today.                                                                                                                                                                                          | Standard (ext_authz)                      |
| I4. Policy document | Lenny and a level 1 proxy                                  | A versioned Lenny JSON document listing each credential identity with its allowed host patterns, ports, and credential verifiers, served by the gateway admin API. Lenny also publishes a plain-text domain list per identity in the External Dynamic List format that security gateways and firewalls ingest over HTTPS. No standard exists for per-identity egress allowlists. NPEP-133 `domainNames` selects pods rather than proxy identities. | Lenny, with EDL output                    |
| I5. Git endpoint          | `netagent` and a git service                                               | git smart HTTP over TLS, protocol v2 for fetch, with Basic or Bearer authentication                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | Standard                                  |
| I6. Upstream chaining | `lenny-egress` and a deployer upstream proxy | Forwarded HTTP CONNECT carrying `Proxy-Authorization: Basic` when the upstream accepts it. Identity is otherwise the egress group's source address. `X-Authenticated-User` and `X-Forwarded-For` are optional, for upstreams that read them on CONNECT or that decrypt. | Standard (de facto for security gateways) |
| I7. Pod binding API       | `netagent` and `lenny-egress-authz`                                        | A Lenny gRPC API over TLS, authenticated by the `lenny-egress` audience token. It covers the binding subscription (including the proxy Service to dial and the covered host set), the base-destination list, `netagent`'s local decision reports, and at level 1 a decision call for each CONNECT.                                                                                                                                                                  | Lenny                                     |
| I8. Pod credential API    | `netagent` and the Token Service; also the mirror on its mTLS identity     | A Lenny gRPC API over TLS, authenticated by the `lenny-token-service` audience token (or the mirror's client certificate). It covers requests for git tokens, binding secrets, federation JWTs, `metadata` credentials, and level 1 proxy credentials, and its responses carry the binding's authoritative hosts and attach template, `cacheUntil`, `handling`, any resolved `requestRules`, and for a `userCredential` binding the expected route target for its list and the CA from the token trust record the Token Service compared. The Token Service consults `lenny-egress-authz` on I3 for the scope decision. | Lenny                                     |

## Configuration by scope

### Scopes and who manages them

| Setting                                                                                                                                              | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | Applies to                                       | Managed by                                                              | Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Components enabled (`netagent`, `lenny-egress`, `lenny-egress-authz`, `lenny-evidence`, `lenny-git-mirror`), images, and replicas                                      | Selects which of the Lenny egress and git components the chart installs, the images they run, and the replica count of each.                                                                                                                                      | Deployment                                       | Platform admin (Helm)                                                   |                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Central proxy implementation: the Envoy default or a replacement address and contract level                                                          | Chooses whether pod egress goes to the shipped Envoy `lenny-egress` to a deployer proxy that calls the decision service (level 2), or to a deployer proxy that enforces a published host list (level 1, not recommended; see "Level 1 replacement").                                                       | Deployment                                       | Platform admin (Helm)                                                   |                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| SSRF exclusion ranges (private, cluster, IMDS, IPv4-mapped, and NAT64)                                                                               | Lists the destination address ranges the central proxy refuses after DNS resolution, so an allowed hostname cannot resolve to an internal address.                                                                                                                | Deployment                                       | Platform admin (Helm)                                                   | They can be extended but never removed below the shipped list.                                                                                                                                       |
| `ipBlock` entries for private upstream proxies and private git services                                                                              | Adds the addresses of private upstream proxies and private git services to the NetworkPolicy of the central proxy, so traffic to them is allowed. Pods receive no such entry at the default level or level 2, and level 1 scopes them by tenant.                                                                                                  | Deployment                                       | Platform admin (Helm)                                                   | NetworkPolicy matches addresses only, so private destinations need these entries.                                                                                                                    |
| Tunnel lifetime menu, and the pool's choice                                                                                                          | The chart renders a menu of `max_stream_duration` values as Envoy routes, and each pool names one. The cap bounds how long a tunnel outlives a revoked lease or an ended session.                                                                                  | Deployment (menu) and pool (choice)              | Platform admin                                                          | The pool's choice must exceed its longest expected stream, such as an LLM completion.                                                                                                                |
| Gates on tenant opt-ins, one per risk class, granted per tenant                                                                        | Grants a tenant the right to turn on the opt-ins of one risk class: stored-token push, agent-readable delivery (`file`, `federation`, and `metadata`), user credential delivery (`file` of a person's token), or verbatim client files.                                                                                                                                                          | Deployment and tenant                            | Platform admin (admin API)                                              | Without the grant, the tenant cannot enable that class's opt-ins.                                                                                                                                            |
| Client proxy profile (default) and CA trust bundle                                                                                                   | Defines the environment variables and file templates that point in-pod tools at the local endpoints, and the CA certificates the pods trust when an upstream intercepts TLS.                                                                                      | Deployment                                       | Platform admin (Helm or admin API)                                      | See "Injected configuration".                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Host sets                                                                                                                                            | Names reusable lists of host patterns and ports, such as `github` or `pypi`, that ceilings, tenant policies, and warm-up policies reference.                                                                                                                      | Deployment (catalog)                             | Platform admin. Lenny ships defaults, and tenants can define their own. | Named lists of host patterns and ports.                                                                                                                                                              |
| Egress upstreams on private addresses, and private git endpoints                                                                                     | Registers proxies and git servers on private addresses, such as a corporate gateway or an internal GitLab, and grants each one to specific tenants.                                                                                                               | Deployment (catalog)                             | Platform admin (admin API), granted to tenants                          | These need `ipBlock` entries in the `lenny-egress` policy. The decision pins their addresses, so the address filter keeps no exemption.                                                              |
| Egress groups | Names additional `lenny-egress` Deployments that the chart renders, each leaving the cluster from a dedicated address, so a corporate gateway or a cloud trust policy can identify a tenant by source address. | Deployment (Helm) for the group, tenant (admin API) for its assignment | Platform admin | The address comes from cloud NAT, a CNI egress gateway, or a dedicated node pool. A granted tenant's pods dial the group's proxy instead of the default (see "Defaults, replacement, and augmentation"). |
| `egressProfile`                                                                                                                                      | Selects the network posture of the pool's pods (`restricted`, `proxied`, or `internet`), which selects the NetworkPolicy each pod receives. `provider-direct` is removed (see "Relationship to the current pool egress configuration").                           | Pool                                             | Platform admin                                                          | A tenant admin sets it only on a pool its tenant owns. Today a tenant admin can change it on a pool shared with other tenants (§10.2 "Manage egress profiles"), and the design restricts that write. |
| Egress ceiling (host sets and patterns, or `*` for any public host)                                                                                  | Sets the largest set of hosts any pod in the pool can reach through the proxy, whichever tenant or session it serves.                                                                                                                                             | Pool                                             | Platform admin                                                          | The outer bound for every tenant using the pool. A tenant overlay can narrow it, and can extend it only when the platform admin allows that for the pool.                                            |
| Warm-up policy (hosts and repositories reachable before binding)                                                                                     | Lists the hosts and repositories a pod can reach while it warms, before it is bound to a tenant or a session.                                                                                                                                                     | Pool                                             | Platform admin                                                          | It covers warm fills, shared assets, and SDK-warm processes. It applies before any tenant is known, so tenants cannot override it. Bound pods use the tenant warm-up additions instead.              |
| Fetch size cap and fetch retry window                                                                                                                | Caps the bytes one fetch may transfer through `netagent` or the mirror, and bounds how long `netagent` retries the mirror for a repository whose first fetch is still running.                                                                                | Pool                                             | Platform admin                                                          | The retry window applies once per initial materialization and must sit below the finalize bound less the Runtime's setup bound; the resume path uses a fixed 30 s wait.                                                             |
| `gitAccess` for `restricted` pools                                                                                                                   | Allows pods in a `restricted` pool to reach the mirror and `lenny-egress`, whose decision allows only the git hosts of the `gitAccess` formula, while all other egress stays closed.                                                                                                                                                  | Pool                                             | Platform admin                                                          | Lets a `restricted` pool reach git endpoints without general egress.                                                                                                                                 |
| Client proxy profile override or extension                                                                                                           | Replaces or extends the deployment's client proxy profile for one Runtime or one pool.                                                                                                                                                                            | Runtime or pool                                  | Platform admin                                                          | For toolchains the default profile misses.                                                                                                                                                           |
| Tenant egress policy: always-allowed hosts, session-requestable hosts, and denied hosts                                                              | Defines the hosts every session of the tenant reaches, the hosts a session can request, and the hosts that are always refused.                                                                                                                                    | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            | Bounded by the pool ceiling.                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Tenant warm-up additions                                                                                                                             | Adds the tenant's own hosts and repositories to warm-up for pods that already belong to the tenant.                                                                                                                                                               | Tenant (tenant-owned pools and pinned idle pods) | Tenant admin                                                            | Bounded by the pool ceiling.                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Egress routes (host patterns to `direct` or an upstream. Hosts that carry an operator's LLM credential are reserved: the check runs at each decision and covers every host set, so a host set added later cannot shadow one, and a deployer's own LLM host is reserved when a pool names it, because a tenant proxy trusted through `tenantOverride.caBundle` could otherwise read the lease)                                                                                              | Maps host patterns to the path the central proxy takes for them: a direct connection, or a chain through a named upstream proxy.                                                                                                                                  | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            | The upstream is one granted by the platform, or one the tenant registered on a public address.                                                                                                       |
| Shadow policy for the tenant egress policy | Lets a tenant admin try a candidate policy. The active policy enforces, and each decision also records what the candidate would have done, in either direction. | Tenant | Tenant admin | It never changes an outcome. Time-limited, 7 days by default, and audited. |
| Egress addresses (read) | Shows the addresses the tenant's traffic leaves from and whether another tenant shares them. | Tenant | Tenant admin | A firewall rule on a shared address also admits other tenants, and the API says so. |
| Effective policy (read) | Shows the computed policy for a pool and optionally a session, with which layer decided each host. | Tenant | Tenant admin | Named permission. |
| Evidence reads and export | Reads the tenant's evidence streams. | Tenant | Tenant admin by default | Per-stream permission. See "Evidence streams". |
| Tenant-registered endpoints on public addresses (upstreams, git endpoints, and other hosts, in one record with a kind, a CA, and kind-specific fields)                                                                                    | Registers upstream proxies and git servers that the tenant operates on public addresses.                                                                                                                                                                          | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            | Resolved and pinned at registration, refused when any address is private or in a registered `ipBlock`, and re-resolved periodically (see "Egress decision service", **Private destinations**).      |
| Credentials for upstream proxies (Basic credential or identity header value)                                                                         | Stores the secret the central proxy presents when it chains a connection to an upstream proxy.                                                                                                                                                                    | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin (`secretData`)                                             |                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Mirror prefetch                                                                                                                                      | Asks the mirror to fetch a repository under one of the tenant's pools ahead of any session, so a large repository's first fetch is paid before a session needs it.                                                                                       | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            | Rate-limited per tenant and bounded by a per-tenant mirror storage quota (P7); refused for repositories the routing rule sends `direct`.                                                                                                                   |
| Git routes (host to the mirror, a git endpoint, or `direct`)                                                                                         | Maps git hosts to the place `netagent` sends their git traffic: the mirror, a registered git endpoint, or the host itself.                                                                                                                                        | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            |                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| VCS credential pools: provider credential, repository prefixes, repository allowlist, `allowWrite`, `allowWorkflowEdits`, and `allowStoredTokenPush` | Defines the provider credential `netagent` uses for each repository prefix, the repositories sessions can use, and whether push, workflow-file edits where the provider has that permission, and stored-token push are allowed.                                                                          | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            | `allowStoredTokenPush` requires the `storedTokenPush` grant.                                                                                                                                         |
| Credential bindings                                                                                                                                  | Defines a secret for any HTTP API or registry (a minted provider token, a stored API key, or a user credential type), the hosts it may be sent to, how it is attached (header and format), whether it attaches to every session or only on request, and its delivery (`inject`, `federation`, `metadata`, or `file`). | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            | Every delivery but `inject` requires its class's grant: `agentReadableDelivery`, or `userCredentialDelivery` for a `userCredential` binding. See "Credential delivery to runtimes".                                                                                      |
| Client config files (for example `.npmrc` with a private registry, or `pip.conf` with an index URL)                                                  | Supplies the tenant's tool configuration files, which `netagent` writes into the pod when the pod is bound to the tenant.                                                                                                                                         | Tenant                                           | Tenant admin                                                            | Written verbatim by `netagent` into `/run/netagent/config/<name>` at binding. A verbatim file is an arbitrary-content channel into the pod, so it needs the `verbatimClientFiles` grant. |
| `gitClone` sources with read or write scope, `writableRepositories`, and workflow-edit requests                                                      | Names the repositories the session clones, the repositories it can push to, and whether it edits workflow files.                                                                                                                                                  | Session                                          | Client                                                                  | Bounded by the tenant's VCS pools and repository allowlist. Rejected at create when out of bounds.                                                                                                   |
| `egress.allowedHosts`                                                                                                                                | Lists the hosts (host sets or patterns) the session has access to. Without it, the session gets the tenant's always-allowed hosts.                                                                                                                                | Session                                          | Client                                                                  | Each entry must lie within the tenant's always-allowed or session-requestable hosts. Not available on concurrent-session pools.                                                                      |
| Credential bindings attached                                                                                                                         | Names the tenant's on-request credential bindings the session uses, and for a minted token optionally a narrower permission set.                                                                                                                                  | Session                                          | Client                                                                  | Bindings marked `always` attach without a request. Not available on concurrent-session pools.                                                                                                        |

Revisions 44 to 49 add settings owned by other designs. `userCredentialDelivery` is one of the gates above, granted per tenant. `userCredentialTypes`, link providers, `requestRules` on a binding, and, under UC-5, `allowRuntimeHeldCredentials` on a VCS pool are tenant settings under `user-credential-delivery-design.md`, and `userAuthorization`, `prAuthoring`, `pinHosts`, and `allowedPushOptions` on a VCS pool are tenant settings under `user-git-identity-design.md`. Presets are deployment data. Each goes through EG-9's write path.

Tenant admins hold most of the configuration surface. The platform admin sets the outer bounds (the ceiling, the SSRF ranges, private-address services, and the opt-in gates) and the settings that must be fixed at pod creation. Tenant admins can register services on public addresses themselves, which follows the connector precedent. Services on private addresses need a platform registration, because they need a NetworkPolicy `ipBlock` on `lenny-egress` and a pinned address in the decision.

A tenant admin holding a platform grant creates derived runtimes and their owned pools, under `tenant-derived-runtimes-design.md`. Every other runtime and pool stays platform-global, and that design's DR-1 removes `tenant-admin` writes on them, changing what §15.1 states today. A template pool supplies the ceiling, the warm-up policy, `tenantEgressProfiles`, and each other setting that needs a platform decision, and the values are copied to the owned pool, so a tenant cannot create a ceiling. `ownerTenantId` comes from draft 0111, which is on the branch `draft/0111-warm-workspace` and has not landed, so EG-9 carries it.

### Tenant pool overlays

Today a pool is one platform-global record. A tenant admin whose tenant has a grant edits that record in place (§10.2, the "own tenant" scoping note), so the edit changes the pool for every tenant that shares it. No per-tenant layer exists: `pool_tenant_access` holds only the grant. Per-tenant override layers exist for other resources, such as the tenant credential policy (§14) and the tenant-level `leaseExtension` override (§8).

The design adds a **tenant pool overlay**: one record per pool and tenant, written by the tenant admin, which applies only to that tenant's pods. The platform admin controls each overridable setting with a per-pool flag, `tenantOverride`, whose values are `none`, `narrow` (the default), and `narrowOrExtend`.

- Narrowing reduces only the tenant's own access, so `narrow` is the default.
- Extending needs `narrowOrExtend`, set by the platform admin per pool and setting.
- A setting applied at pod creation (the NetworkPolicy label, environment variables, and the CA trust bundle) cannot vary per tenant on a shared pool, because warm pods exist before any tenant. The overlay can set those only on a pool the tenant owns (proposal 0111 `ownerTenantId`), where every pod belongs to the tenant from creation.
- The tenant admin's global write on a shared pool is restricted for the egress and git settings below. The tenant admin writes the overlay instead.

| Pool setting | Overlay on a shared pool | Overlay on a tenant-owned pool | Flag |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Egress ceiling | Narrow, or extend when allowed. The extension still excludes the SSRF ranges. | Same | `tenantOverride.egressCeiling` |
| Warm-up policy | None. Unbound pods have no tenant. Bound pods use the tenant warm-up additions. | Replace, within the ceiling | `tenantOverride.warmUp` |
| `gitAccess` | Disable, which the decision service enforces. Enabling needs the NetworkPolicy, which is fixed at creation. | Enable or disable | `tenantOverride.gitAccess` |
| `egressProfile` | None | Choose among the profiles the platform admin lists in `tenantEgressProfiles` | `tenantEgressProfiles` |
| Client proxy profile | File templates only, under the `verbatimClientFiles` grant, because a tenant template is an arbitrary-content channel | On an `internet` pool, environment variables named on an allowlist the chart ships, under "Tenant proxy variables", and on any pool, file templates, with the same grant for templates. A denylist stays open, because `LD_PRELOAD`, `NODE_OPTIONS`, `GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `PATH`, and `LD_AUDIT` all run code. | `tenantOverride.clientProfile` |
| CA trust bundle | None | Add the tenant's CA, such as its gateway's interception CA | `tenantOverride.caBundle` |
| Fetch size cap | Narrow, or extend when allowed | Same | `tenantOverride.fetchSizeCap` |
| Fetch retry window | Narrow | Same, within the pool's validated bound | `tenantOverride.fetchRetryWindow` |

**Tenant proxy variables.** The allowlist holds `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY`, and `NO_PROXY` in both letter cases, `npm_config_proxy`, `npm_config_https_proxy`, `npm_config_noproxy`, `YARN_HTTP_PROXY`, `YARN_HTTPS_PROXY`, and `CARGO_HTTP_PROXY`. The rules are:

- Each proxy value is an `http://` or `https://` URL with a host and an optional port, and `ALL_PROXY` is `http://` only. A URL carrying a user or a password is refused, because launch variables are rendered as arguments.
- A tenant's `NO_PROXY` adds to the list the controller derives and cannot replace it, so a base destination is never sent to the tenant's proxy.
- `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS` is refused, because `-javaagent` and `-XX:OnOutOfMemoryError` run code. The controller renders the JVM proxy properties from the tenant's `HTTPS_PROXY`.
- The trust variables are refused. `caBundle` adds the tenant's CA to the bundle those variables already name.
- A `proxied` or `restricted` pool refuses the variables, as a Runtime `env` entry is refused on a `proxied` pool, because the pod's other sockets are dropped. A tenant on those pools chains through an egress route.

On a pool created under a derivation grant, the rows only a tenant-owned pool has read as `none` unless the grant entry's `ownedOverlays` lists them, because a copied flag was set with the shared pool in mind.

The overlay mechanism is general, and other pool settings, such as scaling, can adopt it later. This design defines it only for the egress and git settings above. Two edges are stated: a platform admin who lowers a flag from `narrowOrExtend` to `narrow` invalidates stored overlays that extend, and the write is refused until those overlays are reduced, except on a pool a derivation grant entry names and on its owned pools, where the write succeeds, the extending part stops applying, and `runtime.base_changed` goes out, because one tenant's overlay must not block a platform write; and enabling `gitAccess` through an overlay on a tenant-owned pool changes the pods' NetworkPolicy label, which takes effect on the next pod roll, so the API reports it as pending until then.

### An open ceiling

A pool ceiling of `*` (any public host) with tenant policies as the effective control is a supported setup. Its safety rests on four points:

- The SSRF exclusions still apply, so `*` covers public addresses only.
- A tenant's policy defaults to the platform's shipped default policy, which allows nothing beyond the default host sets.
- Every connection is decided and logged per tenant, so an open ceiling still produces a complete audit trail.
- What a tenant opens affects only its own pods.

The remaining risk is the exfiltration limit described under "Residual risks and limits". A tenant that allows a host accepting uploads gives its agents a channel to that host.

### Relationship to the current pool egress configuration

| Current setting                                                                                                     | Effect of the design                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `egressProfile: restricted`                                                                                         | Kept. `gitAccess` can add `lenny-git-mirror` and `lenny-egress`, limited by the decision to the `gitAccess` formula's git hosts.                                                                                                                                                          |
| `egressProfile: provider-direct`                                                                                    | Removed. See "Consolidating `provider-direct`" below.                                                                                                                                                 |
| `egressProfile: internet`                                                                                           | Kept for unfiltered egress and non-HTTP protocols. `netagent` can still run in these pools for git credentials and audit, with an added NetworkPolicy rule that allows the in-cluster Lenny services. |
| New `egressProfile: proxied`                                                                                        | Pod egress goes only to the base destinations, the `proxied` resolver, `lenny-egress`, `lenny-egress-authz` (I7), the Token Service (I8), and `lenny-git-mirror`, each on its port.                                                              |
| `deliveryMode`, the base policy, the object store and OTLP policies, and the `egressCIDRs.excludePrivate` and `excludeIMDS` Helm values | Unchanged. `egressCIDRs.providers` goes with `provider-direct`, and `proxied` pods get a per-profile resolver address (see "Residual risks and limits", **DNS channel**). |
| §26.2 "coding runtimes default to `restricted` with an allowlist" and the non-existent `unrestricted` value         | Removed. See "Runtime categories in §26.2" below.                                                                                                                                                     |

### Consolidating `provider-direct`

`provider-direct` works as follows today (§13.2.6):

- It exists for `deliveryMode: direct`, where the pod receives a short-lived LLM provider lease and calls the provider itself. NET-006 pairs `direct` with `provider-direct`, and pairs `proxy` with `restricted`, where the gateway calls the provider.
- The chart renders `allow-pod-egress-provider-direct`, with one `ipBlock` per entry of the Helm value `egressCIDRs.providers` on TCP 443, minus the IMDS addresses (`agent-network-policies.yaml`).
- The list is deployment-wide and holds CIDRs only, because NetworkPolicy cannot match names. The default is empty, so a pod with `provider-direct` and no CIDRs reaches nothing beyond the base destinations.

Its weaknesses are that provider CIDR ranges are wide and shared (a cloud provider's ranges include every tenant's endpoints on that cloud), that they change without notice and are maintained by hand, and that one list serves every pool.

Recommendation: remove it now. No deployment depends on it, so no migration path is needed. `deliveryMode: direct` pairs with `proxied` and a pool ceiling that includes an LLM provider host set (for example `anthropic`, `bedrock`, and `vertex`). The pod then reaches the provider by name, with per-connection decisions and audit, and the tenant overlay can narrow the providers per tenant. The removal touches NET-006 and NET-044; the chart template `agent-network-policies.yaml`, `credentialpool-egress-guard.yaml`, and `admission-policies/direct-mode-isolation-webhook.yaml` with their tests; `egressCIDRs.providers` in `values.yaml`; the admission validators in `pool_config_validator` and `direct_mode_isolation`; the CRD enum and `pkg/embedded/crds`; `pkg/sandbox/egress/profile.go`; `poolstore.go`, `admin/pools.go`, `bootstrap_resources.go`, `sessionserver/credential_preclaim.go`, and `warmpool/controller.go`; preflight; and `tests/tier9_security/pool_admission_isolation_test.go`. LLM traffic through `lenny-egress` is a long-lived streaming CONNECT. The tunnel lifetime cap must exceed the longest LLM stream, or the client retries.

### Runtime categories in §26.2

§26.2 "Shared Patterns for Coding-Agent Runtimes" states that "Coding-agent runtimes default to `egressProfile: restricted` ... HTTPS to the operator-configured allowlist" and that unrestricted egress needs "`egressProfile: unrestricted`". Neither the allowlist nor the value exists. The same section keys other defaults on the coding-agent category: `isolationProfile: sandboxed`, `deliveryMode: proxy`, `setupCommandPolicy`, and `preConnect: false`.

The owner commits of June 2026 (`f79b3b788` and `5b98a6d08`) removed platform enforcement keyed on a coding-agent category, and BUILD-GAPS records that the §26.1 catalog table "carries a documentation-only \"Category\" column". The egress paragraph and the other category-keyed defaults survived that change. No record of the owner's broader feedback that the specification should not define runtime types and their defaults was found.

The design removes the category-keyed prose from §26.2. Platform defaults apply to every Runtime alike, and §5 and §13.2.6 state them. Each shipped reference runtime documents its own `defaultPoolConfig` descriptively in its §26 entry (for example, "the `claude-code` reference runtime ships `egressProfile: proxied` with the host sets `github`, `npm`, and `pypi`"). This change sits in EG-9, and T-26.2.3 closes with it.

## Effective policy

`lenny-egress-authz` computes the effective policy from the binding:

In these formulas, `ceiling` is the pool's egress ceiling with the tenant's pool overlay applied (see "Tenant pool overlays"). Every pool-level setting therefore applies in every case, including concurrent-session pods. Apart from the `gitAccess` case, which replaces the ceiling, the cases differ only in which tenant and session inputs apply.

- **Unbound pod** (warm, before any tenant): `ceiling ∩ pool warm-up policy`, where `ceiling` is the pool's own ceiling, because no overlay applies without a tenant.
- **Tenant-bound pod without a session** (a tenant-owned pool's warm pod, a pinned idle pod, or a service-mode pod): `ceiling ∩ (tenant always-allowed ∪ tenant warm-up additions) − tenant denied`.
- **Pod with one session**: `ceiling ∩ session allowed − tenant denied`, further limited to a binding's hosts and the base destinations when a `userCredential` binding sets `lockEgressToHosts`, which is refused on an `internet` pool and persists for every descendant, whose pool is refused on the same test at delegation. `session allowed` is the session's `egress.allowedHosts` list when the plan sets one, and the tenant's always-allowed hosts otherwise. Each entry of the list must lie within `tenant always-allowed ∪ tenant session-requestable`, or the session is rejected at create.
